<!-- page 1 -->
NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS
Lee Jack Sr. • Jesse Thompson • Jason E. Whiting • Steve Williams • Dawnafe
Whitesinger
"We are Navajo County" 
NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS
Tuesday, March 14, 2017
ONE OR MORE SUPERVISORS MAY ATTEND VIA VIDEO AND/OR
TELEPHONICALLY 
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors will hold a meeting open to the public on Tuesday, March 14, 2017, in the
Supervisors’ Chamber in the Navajo County Governmental Complex, Holbrook, Arizona. The Board may vote to
meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters as indicated on the following agenda. The Board
may meet in executive session pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A) (3) for legal advice on any item.
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the
Board’s office at (928) 524-4053; at least 48 hours prior to the meeting so an accommodation can be arranged.
Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at the Chairman’s discretion. A copy of the agenda background
material provided to the Board, with the exception of items to be considered in executive session, is available for public
inspection at Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
9:00 a.m. Call To Order: Pledge of Allegiance and Invocation
           
1. CALL TO THE PUBLIC: Individuals may address the Board on any relevant issue
for up to 5 minutes. At the close of the call to the public, Board members may not
respond to any comments but may respond to criticism, ask staff to review a matter or
ask that a matter be placed on a future agenda.
 
 
2. CONSENT AGENDA: These items are considered to be routine or administrative in
nature and will be approved in a single motion. A Board member may request for any
item to be removed from the consent agenda and considered on the regular agenda:
 
 
a. Minutes: February 28, 2017 Regular Meeting and February 28, 2017 Executive Session.  
 
b. Warrant & Voucher Report over $1000, February 2017.  
 
c. Constable Reports: Show Low Precinct #5 February and Pinetop-Lakeside Precinct #6
February 2017.
 
 
d. Justice Court Reports: Holbrook Precinct #1, February; Winslow Precinct #2, February;
Snowflake Precinct #3, February; Kayenta Precinct #4, February; Show Low Precinct
#5, February; and Pinetop-Lakeside Precinct #6, February 2017.
 
 
e. Juvenile Probation Report February 2017.  
 
f. Clerk of Superior Court Report February 2017.

<!-- page 2 -->
g. Three year reappointment for Connie Bergstrand, Esther Peterson, Bill Jeffers and
Allison Hephner to serve on the Navajo County Extension Advisory Board beginning
on April 1, 2017.
 
 
h. Adopt Resolution 05-17,supporting Arizona Corporation Commission’s action to
amend the Arizona Universal Service Fund to create a 10% matching fund of
approximately $8,000,000 to match the federal E-rate fund for the state of Arizona and
further urges other rural counties, school districts, cities and towns to support the same. 
 
 
i. Request for Transfer of Grant Funds from Dislocated Worker Program to the Adult
Program, Contract #DI16-002107. 
 
 
j. Memorandum of Understanding between Arizona Department of Economic Security
(ADES), Division of Employment and Rehabilitation Services (DERS), and Navajo
County.
 
 
k. Local Workforce Development Plan (NEAZIWS), July 1, 2017 thru June 30, 2021.  
 
l. Gila River Indian Casino Grant Application for Heber Overgaard Fire District to
replace a 2008 ambulance with a new type II ambulance.
 
 
m. Contracts signed by the County Manager/Assistant County Manager per Board of
Supervisors Resolution: Propane Tank Service Agreement with Owens Energy for the
Navajo County Taylor Road Yard.
 
 
n. Letter of Declaration to renew the Line of Credit for Navajo County.  
 
o. Thank you letter to Navajo Nation Gaming Enterprise for FY17 contribution.  
 
3. PUSH PROGRAM:  
 
a. Presentation to March Wellness Warrior, Maxine Tyler.  
Presented By: Callie Jensen
 
4. ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES:  
 
a. Presentation to retiring employee Peggy Saunders.  
Presented By: Eric Scott/Glenn Kephart
 
b. Consideration and possible approval of Personnel Actions.
Presented By: Eric Scott
 
 
c. Consideration of auction of surplus County items at the end of useful life.  
Presented By: Joyce Alexander

<!-- page 3 -->
d. Economic Development Update. 
Presented By: Paul Watson
 
 
e. Budget Update.
Presented By: Bryan Layton
 
 
5. BOARD OF SUPERVISORS:  
 
a. Presentation by the Arizona Game and Fish Department regarding approaches to
wildlife recreation opportunities in Navajo County and the White Mountains.
 
Presented By: Chris Bagnoli
 
6. PROBATION:  
 
a. Presentation of Share Hope Initiative.  
Presented By: Arno Hall and Lynda Wilson
 
b. Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 06-17, approving
the Family Counseling Grant budget modification to increase FY17 funding.
 
Presented By: Arno Hall
 
7. PUBLIC WORKS:  
 
a. Consideration of award of contract #B17-01-018 Supply and Delivery of Cattle Guards
to Oldcastle Precast, Inc. and W W Cattle Guards, LLC effective March 25, 2017.
 
Presented By: Glenn Kephart
 
b. Consideration of award of contract B17-01-017 Various Roadway Materials to Perkins
Cinders, Inc., Hatch Construction and Paving, Inc., Brimhall Sand and Rock and
Building Materials, Inc., and Cholla Redy Mix, Inc.
 
Presented By: Glenn Kephart
 
8. COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: Report from Board members, County Attorney and
Assistant County Manager.
 
 
 
9. ADJOURN  
 
Dated: March 9, 2017 NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Posted:10:30 am By:MB                         
Melissa W. Buckley - Clerk of the Board

Board of Supervisors Regular 2.g.        
Meeting Date: 03/14/2017  
Title: Cooperative Extension - Advisory Board Appointments
Submitted By: Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Board of Supervisors
Motion before the Board:
Three year reappointment for Connie Bergstrand, Esther Peterson, Bill Jeffers and Allison
Hephner to serve on the Navajo County Extension Advisory Board beginning on April 1, 2017.
Background:
Connie Bergstrand, Esther Peterson, Bill Jeffers and Allison Hephner's terms will be expiring on
April 1, 2017 for the Navajo County Cooperative Extension Advisory Board.  Connie Bergstrand,
Esther Peterson, Bill Jeffers and Allison Hephner have expressed a willingness to continue serving
as Extension Advisory Board Members for an additional three years.
Please reappoint Connie Bergstrand, Esther Peterson, Bill Jeffers and Allison Hephner as
Extension Advisory Board Members for three years effective April 1, 2017.

Board of Supervisors Regular 2.h.        
Meeting Date: 03/14/2017  
Title: E-Rate Universal Service Fund Resolution
Submitted By: Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Board of Supervisors
Motion before the Board:
Adopt Resolution 05-17,supporting Arizona Corporation Commission’s action to amend the
Arizona Universal Service Fund to create a 10% matching fund of approximately $8,000,000 to
match the federal E-rate fund for the state of Arizona and further urges other rural counties,
school districts, cities and towns to support the same. 
Background:
These funds are intended to be used primarily in rural areas of the state for schools and libraries to
provide greater internet access.  These funds are for special construction projects and would not
only provide greater capacity for schools and libraries but also for other users which would greatly
enhance our economic development opportunities in the region.
 
Attachments
E-Rate Universal Service Fund Resolution 
Form Review
Inbox Reviewed By Date

Form Started By: Melissa Buckley Started On: 03/01/2017 02:11 PM
Final Approval Date: 03/01/2017

<!-- page 391 -->
RESOLUTION ____-17 
 
A RESOLUTION OF THE NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS, 
SUPPORTING ARIZONA CORPORATION COMMISSION’S ACTION TO AMEND 
THE ARIZONA UNIVERSAL SERVICE FUND 
 
WHEREAS technology and the digital age are significant drivers of the twenty first century 
economy; and   
 
WHEREAS our youth are our future in meeting the needs of the digital age; and 
 
WHEREAS a robust, reliable and affordable connection to digital infrastructure is in abundance in 
the metropolitan regions of Arizona; and  
 
WHEREAS rural areas of Arizona are significantly underserved by high speed broadband at 
affordable rates; and 
 
WHEREAS said rural areas are at a considerable economic disadvantage due to disparities in 
population densities and geography; and  
 
WHEREAS are youth in rural areas are consequently also at a disadvantage; and  
 
WHEREAS the Arizona Department of Education seeks to obtain federal E -rate funds in order to 
extend high speed broadband connectivity to schools and libraries in rural Arizona ; and  
 
WHEREAS a ten percent match in funding is required in order to obtain the federal E -rate funding 
for the state of Arizona; and  
 
WHEREAS the Arizona Corporation Commission (ACC) is considering an expedited action in 
amending the Arizona Universal Service Fund in order to provide matching funding in obtaining 
the larger federal E-rate grant; and  
 
WHEREAS the action being contemplated by the ACC would have limited duration in that a 
one-time distribution would take place to qualified schools and libraries.  
  
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT R ESOLVED, the Board of Supervisors of Navajo County 
hereby supports the Arizona Corporation Commission’s action to amend the Arizona Universal 
Service Fund to create a 10% matching fund of approximately $8,000,000 to match the federal 
E-rate fund for the state of Arizona and further urges other rural counties, school districts, cities 
and towns to support the same.

Board of Supervisors Regular 2.i.        
Meeting Date: 03/14/2017  
Title: Transfer of Grant Funds-Contract DI16-002107
Submitted By: Carla Fails, Operations Manager
Department: Northeastern Az Innovative Workforce
Motion before the Board:
Request for Transfer of Grant Funds from Dislocated Worker Program to the Adult Program,
Contract #DI16-002107. 
Background:
At the present time 63% of our participants are Adults, with only 11% being Dislocated Workers. 
Transferring the Dislocated Worker Program funding to the Adult Program funding will provide
more training services for the Adults we serve within our three counties.  This will benefit the
local training providers and provide more supportive services to a larger number of Adults.

Section 2:   Summary 
 
Northeastern Arizona Innovative Workforce Solutions Local Workforce Development Area is 
located in the northeastern section of Arizona and is comprised of Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
Counties.  Our local rural area encompasses over 25,973 square miles in the northeast 
corner of Arizona. Portions of the area consist of high desert grasslands, while the northern, 
southern areas have high mountains, including the White Mountains, which is Arizona’s 
second highest mountain range, and desert. Northeastern Arizona Innovative Workforce 
Solutions local area population is 233,068.    
 
The Local Workforce Development Area, named Northeastern Arizona Innovative 
Workforce Solutions, is a three-county Local Workforce Development Area and is part of the 
American Job Center national network. 
 
The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act (WIOA) was signed into law on July 22, 2014 
and became effective July 1, 2015.    The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act of 2014 
supersedes the Workforce Investment Act of 1998 and amends the Adult Education and 
Family Literacy Act, the Wagner-Peyser Act, and the Rehabilitation Act of 1973.   The 
Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act is designed to help youth, job seekers and 
businesses.   The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act requires each Local Workforce 
Development Area to develop and submit to the Governor a comprehensive four-year local 
plan, in partnership with the Chief Elected Officials. 
 
An INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT for Navajo, Apache and Gila County WIOA 
Partnership was fully executed and effective on July 1, 2015.    The AGREEMENT was 
entered into for the purpose of forming a single recognized entity to administer and 
operate programs to serve Adults, Dislocated Workers and Youth under Title I of the Federal 
Workforce Investment Act of 1998 and the newly enacted Federal Workforce Innovation 
and Opportunity Act of 2014.   The AGREEMENT designated Navajo County Board of 
Supervisors to serve in the role of Chief Elected Official, fiscal agent, grant recipient, and 
administrative entity.   
 
A new INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT has been fully executed effective January 1, 
2017 that continues to designate Navajo County to serve in the roles of Chief Elected 
Official, fiscal agent, grant recipient, and administrative entity.   
 
The Workforce Arizona Council envisions a job-driven public workforce development system 
in Arizona that focuses equally on the employment needs of job seekers and employers’ 
needs for skilled labor with industry-recognized credentials to meet their current and future 
demand for labor.   The Workforce Arizona Council has identified twelve (12) characteristics 
that the Local Workforce Development Board embraces:

<!-- page 414 -->
6 
 
1. Extensive Knowledge of Local Economy 
2. Business Engagement 
3. Sector Partnerships 
4. Work-Based Learning Opportunities 
5. Job Seekers Trained for In-Demand Jobs 
6. Industry-Recognized Credentials 
7. Career Pathways 
8. Access to Services 
9. Actively Engage Veterans and Other Priority and Special Populations 
10. Highly Integrated Service Delivery System 
11. Continuous Evaluation and Improvement of Services 
12. Use Evidence-Based, Best and Promising Practices for Service Delivery 
 
Business and community partnerships will be an important strategy in the Local Workforce 
Development Area.  The Local Arizona Workforce Development Board sector analysis will 
focus on new and emerging industries and occupations. 
 
Based on the information provided in Section 3 of this Business Plan  analysis of 
labor market information the targeted “in-demand” industries include: 
Health Care 
Construction 
Utilities 
Manufacturing 
Transportation 
 
The Board’s strategic vision for preparing an educated and skilled workforce inclusive of 
youth and individuals with barriers to employment in order to support the local area 
economic growth and economic self-sufficiency is to build economic success for business 
and workers through workforce development partnerships. 
The Local Workforce Development Board Mission statement is to facilitate business 
retention and growth by partnering with education, service agencies and the business 
community to empower individuals with skills that promote self-sufficiency. 
Strategic Goals: 
 
1. Create a local workforce system that is relevant to business customers. 
2. Establish sector relationships and a career pathway model to grow the talent pipeline of 
new and emerging in-demand industries and occupations. 
3. Embrace a Customer-Center Design that serves diverse populations to improve the 
processes and procedures.  
4. Identify industries that have sustainable wage and career opportunities.

<!-- page 415 -->
7 
 
5. Support dynamic partner alignment and integration to better serve business customers and 
jobseeker clients. 
6. Document evidence-based return on investment through a performance management 
dashboard to track employment, retention, wages, and educational attainment.

SECTION 3:  ANALYSIS 
 
a. Analysis of Regional Economic Conditions 
 
i. Counties covered; brief description of the characteristics of the local area and list 
of service access sites: 
 
The Local Arizona Workforce Development Area includes Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
Counties.   The Local Workforce Development Board has named the local workforce 
system Northeastern Arizona Innovative Workforce Solutions. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area is comprised of a geographically diverse 
region located in northeast Arizona.   The region encompasses 25,973 square miles 
with a population of 233,048 people, 176,498 of whom are over the age of 16.  The 
central portion of the area consists of high desert grasslands, while the northern and 
southern areas have high mountains, including the White Mountains, which is 
Arizona’s second highest mountain range. 
 
Educational attainment in the Local Workforce Development Area varies from the 
statewide statistics. Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties all have a higher rate of high 
school graduates (includes equivalency) than the state; however, there is also a higher 
percentage of individuals who have not completed high school. Post-secondary 
attainment is lower than the rest of the state.  
 
The area’s unemployment rate has steadily decreased following the economic 
recession of 2008-2009, but is still higher than the rest of the state, regardless of 
educational attainment. Although unemployment rates are higher across the state for 
individuals with no more than a high school diploma, the unemployment rates in 
Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties are markedly higher than the state. The largest 
disparity occurs in Navajo and Apache Counties, where the unemployment rate of 
those with a high school diploma is nearly double the state rate (22.5% in Navajo 
County and 21.8% in Apache County vs. 11.0% statewide). 
     
The Local Workforce Development Board will have one Comprehensive 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Center in Globe, Arizona; affiliate ARIZONA@WORK Job 
Centers in Show Low, Payson, Winslow, and Eagar; and a satellite access point in the 
Tonto Basin Public Library.        
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has identified a number of industry sectors 
that are critical to the economic growth of the region. The Local Workforce 
Development Board is comprised of representatives from economic development 
agencies as well as the local business community. The targeted industry sectors were 
determined based on knowledge of current and expected industry trends, a review of 
the regional economic development strategies, as well as a thorough assessment of

<!-- page 418 -->
10 
 
the current labor market statistics report provided by the Arizona Department of 
Administration – Office of Employment and Population Statistics (ADOA OEPS).

<!-- page 419 -->
11 
 
Recent economic development that impacts sector selection and occupations in 
demand: 
 
Manufacturing, which includes our lumber and bio-energy plants, is a thriving 
industry in Navajo and Apache Counties. Currently, the area leadership is working 
with our Congressional Representatives and forest industry workgroups to have more 
access to and expand the use of Forest Service land. According to the Economic 
Development Directors in our region, the majority of the site selectors looking at 
relocating to our area are representing agriculture production companies. These 
companies will require skilled employees trained in advanced manufacturing 
processes to install and maintain production equipment. 
 
There are three power plants in the Local Workforce Development Area. Combined, 
they are the largest employers in Apache County and the third largest in Navajo 
County. While they are not showing an increase in the number of new jobs in the 
statistics provided by ADOA EPS, it is expected that a large number of employees 
will be retiring and that new employees will need to be hired and trained. With the 
advent of new technology within the energy industry, employees may need to receive 
incumbent worker training in order to maintain their current positions. 
 
The Health Care Industry is projected to have employment gains of 2.9% during the 
2012-2022 timeframe according to the ADOA EPS data. Based on recent and planned 
hospital expansions in the area, it is anticipated that employer demand for skilled 
employees in all in-demand health care occupations will be much more substantial. 
Cobre Valley Regional Medical Center in Gila County completed a 66,810 sq. ft. 
expansion project in Fall 2016 which increased the number of inpatient beds in 
multiple departments and also included a new pharmacy, cath lab, wound center, and 
physician offices. Summit Regional Medical Center in Navajo County is scheduled to 
break ground on a $50 million expansion project in May, 2017. The project includes 
an outpatient center that will include multiple specialty physicians and increase the 
types of services available to area residents. Physicians moving into both the Gila 
County and Navajo County areas will require skilled front and back office staff 
including trained Medical Assistants, Certified Nursing Assistants, Registered 
Nurses, and Medical Secretaries.

Navajo and Apache – Designated as a high poverty area for Youth 
 
Youth living in high poverty areas are automatically considered low-income 
individuals. WIOA defines a high poverty area is a census tract, set of continuous 
census tracks, Indian reservation, tribal land, or Native Alaskan Village or county that 
has a poverty rate of at least 30 percent as set every five (5) years using the American 
Community Survey 5-year data. Two counties in Arizona, Navajo and Apache 
Counties, have been identified with a poverty rate higher than 30 percent.  
 
b. An analysis of the knowledge and skills needed to meet the employment needs of the 
employers in the region, including employment needs in in-demand industry sectors 
and occupations (an existing analysis may be used, per 108(c) of WIOA); 
 
Basic Skills: There is a recognized high demand for GED preparation services and 
remedial education in the Local Workforce Development Area and is often the first step 
for participants seeking employment along the career pathways in the targeted industries.  
Within the In-Demand industry sectors, Adult Education will play a large role in 
providing services for job seekers to meet the needs of the employer.

<!-- page 423 -->
15 
 
Soft Skills: There is a recognized need for training in soft skills as job postings in the 
region for the first half of 2016 indicate employers are specifically looking for employees 
who exercise proper workplace etiquette. 
 
Supervisory Skills: Many of the Occupations In-Demand for the targeted industries are 
First-Line Supervisory level positions. This is a skill-set area that has potential for 
incumbent worker training and could be used as part of an up-skill, back-fill strategy. 
 
Career and Technical Education (CTE):  Many of the In-Demand Occupations in the 
targeted industries in the Local Workforce Development Area require industry 
recognized credentials and vocational skills. Training providers in the area work closely 
with employers to continually update program offerings to coincide with current 
employer and industry needs. 
 
Industrial Skilled Trades: It is anticipated that as Baby Boomers in the energy industry 
continue to retire, there will be an increased need to fill the talent pipeline in Industrial 
Maintenance and Operations.  
 
Skilled Construction Trades: According to the data provided by ADOA-EPS, the 
Construction industry is one of the fastest-growing industries in the region. There will be 
a continued need for employees skilled in various construction trades. 
 
Healthcare Trades: The ADOA-EPS analysis identified high demand for healthcare 
practitioners at all levels – entry level, technician and professionals.  
 
Meeting the workforce demands of the business community requires an alignment of 
economic development, education (including post-secondary), and workforce 
development efforts in the local LWDA strategies.   The Local Workforce Development 
Area will participate in monitoring ongoing assessments of not only the state’s current 
economic environment, but the county’s contribution to it as well. It will be critical 
during the lifespan of this plan to closely monitor the economic direction of the county’s 
business attraction efforts. Targeted industries identified by the county along with current 
in-demand industries and the occupations within those industries are the drivers for the 
workforce activities. 
 
c. An analysis of the workforce in the region, including current labor force 
employment (and unemployment) data, and information on labor market trends, 
and the educational and skill levels of the workforce in the region, including 
individuals with barriers in employment: 
 
The unemployment rate in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties has been steadily 
decreasing since 2010; however, as the chart below indicates, at the end of 2015 the rates 
for all three counties (9.7% in Navajo, 13.4% in Apache, and 8.0% in Gila) are 
significantly higher than the state unemployment percentage of 6.1%.

<!-- page 424 -->
16 
 
 
 
According to the Arizona Department of Administration – Employment and Population 
Statistics, unemployment during 2016 continued to drop but Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
Counties are still experiencing unemployment at a significantly higher rate than the 
statewide percentage. As of October 2016, Arizona is at 5.2% unemployment; Navajo 
County is at 7.7%, Apache County is at 11.0%, and Gila County is at 7.1%.  
 
Unemployment rates by educational attainment (Ages 25-64) 
 
An ADOA EPS analysis shows that unemployment decreases significantly with higher 
levels of education. Unemployment levels in the region for those individuals without a 
high school diploma are as much as double the state percentage. However, for all levels 
of educational attainment up to earning an Associate’s Degree, Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
County residents experience higher levels of unemployment than the statewide level.

The ADOA EPS Chart below highlights a number of characteristics of the Northeastern

Household Income: The area’s median household income is 20%-35% lower than the 
state’s median income. 
 
Poverty Status: Poverty is significantly more prevalent in the Local Workforce 
Development Area than the rest of the state. As previously mentioned, Navajo and 
Apache Counties are the only two counties in Arizona designated as high poverty areas.

d. An analysis of the workforce development activities (including education and 
training) in the region, including an analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of such 
services, and the capacity to provide such services, to address the identified 
education and skill needs of the workforce and the employment needs of employers 
in the region. 
 
Given the high percentage of residents in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties who lack a 
high school diploma (or its equivalent), adult basic education is a priority in the region. 
Northland Pioneer College is the Adult Basic Education provider in Navajo and Apache 
Counties, while the Gila County Superintendent of Schools provides these services in 
Gila County.  The Local Workforce Development Area works closely with the adult basic 
education providers to support participants in earning their General Education Diploma 
(GED).  
 
Northland Pioneer College and Gila Community College play an important role in 
providing post-secondary training needed within the Local Workforce Development 
Area, offering areas of study to gain the skills, knowledge, or certification needed to 
begin or enhance a career forecasted to be an in-demand industry or occupation.  Both 
colleges currently offer trainings for the following in-demand health care occupations: 
Registered Nurses, Medical Assistants, and Medical Secretaries. These community 
colleges also work with local employers to provide clinical experience for the 
students. 
 
Northland Pioneer College offers training in both Mechatronics and Industrial 
Maintenance and Operations. These programs prepare students for employment in in-
demand occupations in the Manufacturing and Utilities industries. 
 
For participants seeking training for in-demand occupations in the transportation 
industry, participants will be guided to the Eligible Training Provider List to find a 
training provider that will provide the skills, knowledge and certification needed. 
 
There is a need to coordinate resources to expand on-the-job training programs and 
accelerate the pipeline of qualified workers to open positions in Construction and 
Manufacturing in-demand occupations and the Local Workforce Development Board will 
work closely with local employers to design and implement on-the-job trainings not only 
for positions that do not require formal training but will also work to enhance on-the-job 
training opportunities to ensure that students completing approved training programs 
have access to additional employer-specific training and local employment. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has worked with the educational institutions in 
the three-county area to build the skills and knowledge of jobseekers.    Like all rural 
areas, Northeastern Arizona is limited by the number of available certificate and/or 
credential opportunities both stackable and portable.  Any program of study on the

<!-- page 428 -->
20 
 
Arizona Job Connection Eligible Training Provider List, On-the-Job Training, and 
Apprenticeships can be offered to gain the skills, knowledge, or certification needed to 
begin or enhance a career forecasted to be an in-demand industry or occupation. The 
Local Workforce Development Board’s focus on specific industries and occupations will 
provide a blueprint for creation of credentials and certifications available through local 
training providers.

Section 4:  Goals and Strategies 
 
a. A description of the local board’s strategic vision and goals for preparing an educated 
and skilled workforce (including youth and individuals with barriers to employment), 
including goals relating to the performance accountability measures based on primary 
indicators of performance described in section 116(b)(2)(A) in order to support 
regional economic growth and economic self-sufficiency; and,  
The Local Workforce Development Board’s strategic vision for preparing an educated and 
skilled workforce inclusive of youth and individuals with barriers to employment in order 
to support the local area economic growth and economic self-sufficiency is: 
To build economic success for business and workers through workforce 
development partnerships. 
The Local Workforce Development Board Mission Statement is: 
 Facilitate business retention and growth by partnering with education, 
service agencies and the business community to empower individuals 
with skills that promote self-sufficiency 
Strategic Goals: 
 
1. Create a local workforce system that is relevant to business customers. 
2. Identify industries that have sustainable wage and career opportunities. 
3 Establish sector relationships and a career pathway model to grow the talent 
pipeline of new and emerging in-demand industries and occupations. 
4 Embrace a customer-center design that serves diverse populations and provides 
seamless client customer-focused service delivery network.  
5 Support partner alignment and integration to better serve business customers and 
jobseeker clients. 
6. Deliver effective case management services to assess skill gaps and barriers to 
employment including intake services, assessment and developing and 
implementing individual employment strategy. 
7. Collaborate among workforce and stakeholder partners to provide client-centered 
initiatives that leverage resources and services toward the client’s successful entry 
or reentry into the workforce. 
8 Document evidence-based return on investment through a performance 
management dashboard to track employment, retention, wages, and educational 
attainment.

<!-- page 430 -->
22 
 
b. Taking into account analyses described in subparagraphs (A) through (D), a strategy to 
work with the entities that carry out the core programs to align resources available to 
the local area, to achieve the strategic vision and goals described in subparagraph (E). 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has established a team comprised of core 
program partners to provide recommendations and advice on the local workforce 
system and to establish procedures for aligning resources.    The team has discussed 
aligned procedures that will enhance the Local Workforce Development Area’s ability to 
carry out the vision and goals for the ARIZONA@WORK System: 
 
 Ensure that ALL jobseeker clients, regardless of where they first access the 
workforce system, will be registered in the ARIZONA@WORK database.  
 Establish initial screening questions to help in identifying potential partner 
engagement. 
 Establish partner lists of typical characteristics for each program in regard to 
potential eligibility and suitability for enrollment to assist with targeted referrals.     
 Each partner has negotiated a referral process that kicks in based on the initial 
screening findings and has agreed that if the initial screening is done at the 
American Job Center, basic individualized services will be offered and provided. 
 Create a service map of available basic and individualized career services, training, 
and supportive services.     
 Create a system orientation to be used at all access points in the Local Workforce 
Development Area. 
 Initiate a primary case management system where an individual maintains his or her 
case manager at the point of first enrollment to provide a single point of contact 
regardless of the subsequent funding streams he or she may be co-enrolled in.    
Subsequent funding streams will serve as a program contact that maintains 
appropriate recordkeeping to track progress and performance data. 
 Establish a tracking form that will be used until such time a statewide electronic 
tracking is available via an integrated case management system. 
 Core Program Partners will meet at least every two months to review referral 
listings, enrollments, co-enrollments, and exits.    These meetings will serve as 
informational to help partners follow through on referrals, to provide opportunities 
for pitching co-enrollment possibilities, discuss performance strategies, and to 
coordinate exits. 
 Core Program Partners that have employment as a planned outcome have agreed to 
participate on an integrated business service team including Adults, Dislocated 
Workers, Youth, Wagner-Peyser, Vocational Rehabilitation, and Adult Education. 
 Core Program Partners, local Community Colleges, and Economic Development 
Representatives will assist in the support of sector relationships for the identified 
existing and emerging industries and occupations.   The sector relationships will 
incorporate career pathways into its responsibilities in terms of creating a flow of 
the career path options, analysis of the needs and expectations of business

<!-- page 431 -->
23 
 
customers, analysis of the existing skills and knowledge of the talent pipeline, and 
work with education representatives to tap into existing training or create new 
credentialing or certification options.    
 Core Program Partners have discussed and agreed upon the existing and emerging 
in-demand industries and occupations that the Local Workforce Development Area 
will focus on regarding business development and the building of a talent pipeline to 
meet business needs and expectations.

Section 5:  Workforce System and Partners 
 
A description of the workforce development system in the local area that identifies the 
programs that are included in that system and how the local board will work with the 
entities carrying out core programs and other workforce development programs to 
support alignment to provide services.   Discuss how the LWDA will align polices, 
operations, administrative systems and other procedures to assure coordination and 
avoid duplication of the workforce programs and activities.   Describe agreements and 
procedures in place or in progress, and describe how the local board will work towards 
WIOA compliance in regard to the selection of a one-stop operator and a funding 
mechanism for one-stop infrastructure costs (see WIOA sec 121 and draft regulations Part 
678).   Also include a link to where policies and procedures are posted and provide copies 
of existing agreements in the appendix.   
 
The Local Workforce Development Area is a three-county area including Navajo, Apache, 
and Gila Counties.  The Local Workforce Development Area is fortunate to have had long 
standing relationships with the core and required partners identified in the Workforce 
Innovation and Opportunity Act and a history of working together for the good of the 
communities they serve.   The Local Workforce Development Area is named Northeastern 
Arizona Innovative Workforce Solutions and is part of the American Job Center national 
network. 
The Board of Supervisors in the three-counties designated the Navajo County as fiscal 
agent, grant recipient, and administrative entity for the Local Workforce Development Area.  
In June 2015 an Intergovernmental Agreement setting forth the fiscal relationships between 
Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties under the Local Workforce Development Area was 
approved.  This agreement provided that Navajo County is designated by Navajo County, 
Apache County, and Gila County  Board of Supervisors to serve as Chief Elected Official, 
fiscal agent, and administrative entity for Northeastern Arizona Innovative Workforce 
Solutions (Appendix B). 
 
In November 2016, a Partnership Agreement was created between the Chief Elected Official 
and the Local Workforce Development Board.  The Local Workforce Development Board has 
been designated in accordance with the requirement in the Workforce Innovation and 
Opportunity Act to specify the relationship between the Chief Elected Official and the Local 
Workforce Development Board (Appendix C).  
 
In November 2016, a draft Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) was submitted to the 
Workforce Arizona Council.   This MOU purpose is to improve the accessibility and quality of 
services made available to a partner shared customer pool (Appendix D).

<!-- page 433 -->
25 
 
Navajo County, as Chief Elected Official, appointed a diverse group based on the 
recommendations received from Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties to serve on the Local 
Workforce Development Board that is representative of core program partners as well as 
business and industry (particularly existing and emerging in-demand industries and 
occupations), union representation, and educational leaders.   Core program partners, 
required partners, and other community-based organizations serve on the partnering group 
that advises the Board on issues associated with alignment and integration of services.       
The Board has five active committees including:  Executive, Business Services, One-Stop 
Services, Disabilities, and Youth: 
1. Executive Committee shall meet as necessary between scheduled meetings of the entire 
Local Workforce Development Board membership and shall be responsible for 
interpreting and putting into effect the decisions, policies and programs of the Local 
Workforce Development Board.  If a Local Workforce Development Board meeting has 
been cancelled and action items are needed, the Executive Committee shall put into 
effect and vote on the action items.  All actions are subject to ratification of the full 
Local Workforce Development Board at its next regularly scheduled meeting. 
 
2. Business Services Committee shall oversee the delivery of employer services and 
provide guidance and direction to designated staff.  The committee will review and 
interpret labor market and sector data to assist in identifying the needs and 
expectations of business and industry in the three county area. 
 
3. ARIZONA@WORK Job Center Services Committee shall oversee the service delivery and 
partner collaboration within the one-stop centers and review and provide guidance on 
the one-stop certification requirements to ensure all basic and career services are 
available in the one-stop center. 
 
4. Disability Services Committee shall be responsible for the monitoring of the Americans 
with Disabilities Act (ADA) to ensure the accessibility of its One-Stops to all persons with 
disabilities.   
 
5. Youth Services Committee shall provide guidance and direction on WIOA Title 1 youth 
services in the three county area. 
The Local Workforce Development Board in partnership with the Chief Elected Official 
selected Navajo County to serve as the Title I Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Provider.  
The Local Workforce Development Area Executive Director functionally reports to the Local 
Workforce Development Board and reports to James Jayne, Navajo County Manager.  The 
Local Workforce Development Area Executive Director serves as support to the Local 
Arizona Workforce Development Board.  Details are provided within this plan regarding 
arm’s length decision-making and assurance that no conflict of interest will be real or

<!-- page 434 -->
26 
 
perceived in the procurement of a One-Stop Operator or related to monitoring and 
oversight of programs.     
All core partners and additional partners who provide services in the Local Workforce 
Development Area are part of the local workforce system.  Funding streams that represent 
core and required partners include:    
 Community Services Block Grant 
Services low-income individuals to empower, educate, coordinate and assist with reducing 
poverty and building self-sufficiency.  Gila County Community Action Program is the 
provider in Gila County and Northern Arizona Council of Governments is the provider in 
Navajo and Apache Counties. 
 Older Americans Act 
Services older adults through Senior Community Service Employment Program with 
employment and training assistance.  The Arizona Agency on Aging is the provider. 
 Temporary Assistance for Needy Families 
Services individuals who have dependent children with cash assistance when available 
resources do not fully address the family’s needs while preparing program participants for 
independence through work.  Arizona Department of Economic Security is the provider. 
 Title I Adult 
Services target individuals who are 18 and older and who have been chronically 
unemployed or underemployed, have barriers to getting and keeping a job, and need 
assistance with job search and/or training.  Navajo County is the current service provider. 
 Title I Dislocated Worker 
Services target individuals who have lost their jobs due to closure or downsizing through no 
fault of their own.  Navajo County is the current service provider. 
 Title I Youth 
Services target older youth 18 years to 24 years of age who have barriers to getting and 
keeping a job and are in need of educational and employment services. Navajo County is 
the current service provider.   
 Title II Adult Education and Literacy 
Services target individuals in need of basic education classes with low basic skills and low 
educational attainment; English language acquisition for eligible learners to achieve 
competence in reading, writing, speaking and comprehension of the English language; and 
math skills needed to attain a high school credential and transition to post-secondary 
education and employment.    Gila County and Northland Pioneer College are the current 
service providers.    
 Title III Wagner Peyser 
Services target individuals who are in need of help with updating or developing a resume 
and labor exchange assistance that connects them to employment.  Arizona Department of 
Economic Security is the service provider.  
 Title IV Rehabilitation Act 
Services target individuals with disabilities and as per the WIOA will be treated mainstream 
when accessing the American Job Centers.  Arizona Department of Economic Security is the 
provider.   
 Trade Act

<!-- page 435 -->
27 
 
Services individuals who are eligible workers who lose their jobs, or whose hours of work 
and wages are reduced, as a result of increased imports or production transfers abroad.  
Arizona Department of Economic Security is the provider. 
 
 Unemployment Compensation 
Services individuals who are unemployed and eligible to receive unemployment 
compensation.  Arizona Department of Economic Security is the provider.    
 Veterans Program (Jobs for Veterans State Grant Program) 
Services individuals who are veterans with significant barriers to employment to receive 
tailored employment and training services.  Arizona Department of Economic Security is 
the provider. 
Funding streams not available in the local area include:    
 Employment and Training Housing and Urban Development 
 Migrant and Seasonal Farmworkers 
 Second Chance Act 
 Youth Build 
The Local Workforce Development Area embraces the concepts outlined in Arizona’s State 
Plan regarding alignment and integration.  The Local Workforce Development Board has 
convened teams comprised of all of the workforce system partners as well as economic 
development.  The group has been tasked with aligning and integrating services to provide a 
comprehensive approach to serving business customers, job seekers, and youth.  This plan 
will outline strategies that have been created by the team in the areas of initial screening, 
workforce system registration, resource room coverage, assessment, testing, referrals, 
workshops, and co-enrollments. 
a. An identification of the entity responsible for the disbursal of grant funds described in 
section 107(d)(12)(B)(i)(III), as determined by the chief elected official or the Governor 
under section 107(d)(12)(B)(i). 
 
The Boards of Supervisors in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties, in their 
Intergovernmental Agreement, designated Navajo County to serve as Chief Elected 
Official, fiscal agent, and administrative entity responsible for the disbursal of grant 
funds. 
 
b. A description of the competitive process to be used to award the sub-grants and 
contracts in the local area for activities carried out under this title. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board is not incorporated.   Navajo County was 
designated the fiscal agent, grant recipient, and administrative entity by the Chief 
Elected Official in the Intergovernmental Agreement.  In its capacity as administrative 
entity, Navajo County serves as the Local Workforce Development Board support and 
assists the Board in fulfilling its duties.

<!-- page 436 -->
28 
 
Navajo County Department of Procurement is responsible for the procurement of 
goods and services in accordance with the Navajo County Procurement Code, Arizona 
Revised Statutes and Federal law.   In line with the Workforce Innovation and 
Opportunity Act, Local Workforce Development Board, the Chief Elected Official, and 
by virtue of approval of this Plan, the Governor of Arizona, have selected and approved 
Navajo County to serve as service provider for the Title I Adult, Dislocated Workers, and 
Youth Programs.     
 
c. A description of the roles and resource contributions of the one-stop partners. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board is dedicated to a fully integrated and efficient 
Job Center Service Delivery System.  The Comprehensive ARIZONA@WORK Job Center 
partners have negotiated and signed a Memorandum of Understanding along with 
required partners that includes roles and resource contributions to achieve their goal of 
seamless services. 
 
Programs and services must be tiered according to customer levels and employment 
readiness.  Customers enter the ARIZONA@WORK System at varying degrees of job 
readiness for employment and therefore require different programs and services based 
on their individual needs.  ARIZONA@WORK System partners will work together to 
provide staff, programs, and resources in a manner that enables customers to be 
provided with seamless delivery of services regardless of funding stream. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board intends to align with Governor Doug Ducey’s 
vision for Arizona, to build a pro-growth economy that provides opportunity for all and 
creates prosperous communities. This means ensuring that all Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
County citizens eligible to work, regardless of their circumstance, background, social 
status or zip code, have access to the best economic opportunity, educational options, 
healthcare services, safety system and overall quality of life as possible. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board realizes that different populations present 
unique challenges. The Core Partners which consist of WIOA Title l Adult, Dislocated 
Worker Program, Title ll Adult Education, Title lll Employment Services, Title lV 
Vocational Rehabilitation, and the ARIZONA@WORK System are each strategically 
positioned to serve a very broad audience; this allows the ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers 
to serve a wide variety of individuals with programs and knowledgeable staff targeted to 
overcome their specific barriers to employment. In the Job Centers the Welcome Team 
will continue to conduct the initial interview triage to determine how customers’ needs 
are best met and will make appropriate referrals to core partners and community 
resources.  Core Partner staff will be cross-trained so that all will have a good 
understanding of services available for specific populations and a referral process will be 
established.

<!-- page 437 -->
29 
 
In compliance with Workforce Innovative and Opportunity Act regulations Board staff will 
work with Navajo County Procurement Department to create a Request for Proposal (RFP) 
for a One-Stop Operator. The role of the One Stop Operator (20 CFR 678.620) will 
coordinate the service delivery of required ARIZONA@WORK Job Center partners and 
service providers.  
 
Title I Workforce Development Programs include the Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth 
Programs. The Adult Program provides adults age 18 and older a variety of workforce 
activities designed to increase employment, retention, earnings, and attainment of 
recognized postsecondary credentials. The Dislocated Worker Program provides services to 
adults age 18 and older who have been terminated, laid off, or have received notice of 
termination or layoff from employment (through no fault of their own) generally due to 
closures or downsizing. Self-employed individuals and individuals who meet the WIOA 
definition of a displaced homemaker may also be eligible for services. These services include 
basic career services, individualized career services and training services. Priority of Service 
for the Adult Program for individualized career and training services includes veterans, 
recipients of public assistance, low income individuals, and individuals who are basic skills 
deficient. 
 
The Title l Youth Program provides services to in-school youth (Ages 14-21) and Out-of- 
School Youth (ages 16-24) with barriers to employment such as being school dropouts, 
subjects of the adult justice system, basic skills deficient, English Language Learners, 
pregnant and parenting, and/or youth with a disability. Seventy-five percent of all youth 
funds must be used to support out-of-school youth. 
 
Title ll Adult Education under WIOA is to enable local adult education providers, as core 
partners of the ARIZONA@WORK Workforce System, to develop, implement and improve 
adult education and literacy services throughout the state. Individuals eligible for Adult 
Education services are those individuals who are 16 years old or older, are not enrolled or 
required to be enrolled in school, are basic skills deficient, lack a secondary diploma, or are 
English language learners. 
 
Title lll Employment Service provides a network of public employment offices that offer 
placement services for jobseekers and labor force recruitment services for employers. 
Coordination of service delivery and information sharing will offer better customer service 
and avoid duplication of services.  Title lll and Title l will share the same statewide database. 
 
Title lV Vocational Rehabilitation (VR), authorized by Congress through the Rehabilitation 
Act of 1973, as amended, is now part of the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act.  
The purpose of the Vocational Rehabilitation program is to assist individuals with disabilities 
to prepare for, obtain, advance, and maintain competitive, integrated employment.

<!-- page 438 -->
30 
 
d. Employment Service.   A description of plans and strategies for, and assurances 
concerning, maximizing coordination of services provided by the State employment 
service under the Wagner-Peyser Act 929 U.S.C. 49 et seq.) and services provided in 
the local area through the one-stop delivery system, to improve service delivery and 
avoid duplication of services. 
 
Wagner-Peyser is a core program partner in the ARIZONA@WORK Job Center Delivery 
System and management has been actively involved in the partner work done to 
prepare this plan, service map, identify areas of collaboration, and build day-to-day 
operational procedures.    
Wagner-Peyser will be co-located full-time in the Comprehensive ARIZONA@WORK Job 
Center and share in the facility costs.   In addition, a representative will set office hours 
in our affiliate ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers. 
Along with the other core program partners, Wagner-Peyser has agreed to the customer 
flow that provides for a “no wrong door” approach to serving clients in the Local 
Workforce Development Area.   The design has all core program partner clients 
registering in the ARIZONA@WORK database and viewing a system orientation as well 
as receiving any basic career services available at the location they access.    Wagner-
Peyser has also agreed to the referral process and initial screening to identify potential 
services for all Center clients.    
Wagner-Peyser, along with partner staff, will continue to provide front-door coverage, 
resource room coverage, and basic career service workshops open to all workforce 
system clients.    Wagner-Peyser will participate on the local integrated Business 
Services Team to ensure employers are served according to a customer center design 
and that partners are not duplicating efforts. 
 
e. Adult Education.   A description of how the local board will coordinate workforce 
investment activities carried out under this title in the local area with the provision of 
adult education and literacy activities under title II in the local area, including a 
description of how the local board will carry out, consistent with subparagraphs (A) 
(B)(i) of section 107(d)(11) and section 232, the review of local applications submitted 
under title II. 
 
Adult Education and Literacy is a core program partner and has been actively 
participating in the partner convening groups held in the Local Workforce Development 
Area to prepare this plan and service map, identify areas of collaboration, and build day-
to-day operational procedures.  
The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act focuses on targeted populations with 
more barriers than previous legislations.    The Local Workforce Development Area is 
expecting to “meet people where they are” and help individuals gain the skills and 
knowledge they need to get and keep a job.    The sector focused approach provides a

<!-- page 439 -->
31 
 
blueprint for career pathways.    Individuals will enter the system with the partner that 
they will most benefit from and begin a path toward self-sufficiency via career 
pathways.   The career pathways model will create a talent pipeline to fill employment 
in industries and occupations that are forecasted to have growth and provide a self-
sufficient wage over the next few years.   The Local Workforce Development Board 
believes that many of the workforce system individuals will start with Adult Basic 
Education or English as a Second Language and build from there.   The partnership with 
Adult Basic Education and Literacy is essential.     
Along with the other core program partners, Adult Education and Literacy in all three (3) 
counties has agreed to the customer flow that provides for a “no wrong door” approach 
to serving individuals in the Local Workforce Development Area.   The design has all core 
program partner clients registering in the ARIZONA@WORK database and viewing a 
system orientation as well as receiving any basic career services available at the location 
they access.    Adult Education and Literacy has also agreed to the referral process and 
initial screening to identify potential services for all American Job Center jobseekers.    
The Adult Education and Literacy provider for Gila County is located in the 
Comprehensive ARIZONE@WORK Job Center.  They are conducting GED and ESL classes, 
testing, and tutoring.  A referral process is in place to capture individuals to co-enroll.    
This is of particular importance in the Comprehensive ARIZONA@WORK Job Center 
located in Globe, Arizona. 
The Adult Education and Literacy Providers expect a request for proposal package 
available in January 2017.  Prior to submission, the Local Workforce Development Board 
or its Executive Committee will review the proposals and provide comments.  The 
Executive Committee has the authority to conduct business between Local Workforce 
Development Board meetings.   A special Executive Committee meeting will be held to 
accommodate the review process and allow the local providers the time they need to 
complete their proposals prior to the review.    
f. Vocational Rehabilitation.   A description of the replicated cooperative agreements (as 
defined in section 107(d)(11) between the local board or other local entities described 
in section 101(a)(11)(B) of the Rehabilitation Act of 1973 (29 U.S.C. 721(a)(11)(B)) and 
the local office of a designated State agency or designated State unit administering 
programs carried out under title I of such Act (29 U.S.C. 720 et seq.) (other than 
section 112 or part C of that title (29 U.S.C. 732,741) and subject to section 121(f) in

to other individuals, such as cross training of staff, technical assistance, use and 
sharing of information, cooperative efforts with employers, and other efforts at 
cooperation, collaboration, and coordination.
