32 
 
The Arizona Department of Economic Security – Rehabilitation Services, in accordance 
with 29 U.S.C. 721(a)(11) will provide the following services to individuals with 
disabilities:  
 
 Provide intake, orientation, and assessments for disabled job-seekers 
 Promote employment of persons with disabilities 
 Provide comprehensive assessments and an Individualized Plan for Employment for 
those who are both eligible to receive services and meet the Division’s Order of 
Selection criteria 
 Provide guidance and counseling, physical restoration, and training to financially 
eligible persons with disabilities 
 Provide follow-up services to enhance job retention 
 Provide other services as may be available and appropriate 
 Provide Pre-Employment Transitioning Services for students with disabilities, as 
defined by the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act 
 Provide Supported Employment Service for youth with disabilities as defined by the 
Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act  
 Provide independent living services for older blind individuals to enhance the 
capacity of persons with disabilities to live unaided in the community 
 Provide performance information as required by the Workforce Innovation and 
Opportunity Act 
 Provide cross training of Workforce staff on disability related issues 
 Provide technical assistance on disability related issues and on assistive technology 
 Engage employers through the Division’s Business Liaisons 
 
Vocational Rehabilitation provides services once a week in the Comprehensive 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Center.  The Vocational Rehabilitation Service Specialists 
coordinate services and referrals among the Workforce Partners for individuals with 
disabilities.  
 
g. Programs of study authorized under the Carl D. Perkins Career and Technical 
Education Act of 2006 (20 U.S.C. 2301 et seq.), that support the strategy identified in 
the State plan under section 102(b)(1)(E). 
 
Carl D. Perkins Career and Technical Education Act programs services are provided at 
Northland Pioneer College.  This program provides students with strong experience in 
and understanding of all aspects of an industry, including partnerships/collaboration 
with local industries.  Programs of Study consist of: Automotive Technology, 
Computer/Information Systems, Construction Technology, Industrial Maintenance & 
Operations, Mechatronics, Medical Assistant, Nursing, Nursing Assistant, 
Paramedicine/EMT, and Welding which are In-Demand Industry Occupations.

<!-- page 441 -->
33 
 
h. Secondary and Postsecondary Education.   A description of how the local board will 
coordinate education and workforce investment activities carried out in the local area 
with relevant secondary and postsecondary education programs and activities to 
coordinate strategies, enhance services, and avoid duplication of services. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has established a Youth Committee to ensure 
that youth workforce activities are focused on the needs and expectations specific to 16 
to 24 year olds.   The Committee is chaired by a voting member of the Local Workforce 
Development Board. The Committee is tasked to ensure youth services are coordinated 
between education and workforce development activities, particularly in regard to 
postsecondary education.   This should ensure avoiding any duplication of services.  The 
engagement with secondary education includes recruitment efforts targeting individuals 
who have dropped out of school or who are graduating and need help with further 
education and training activities. 
Representatives from Adult Education and Vocational Rehabilitation sit on the Local 
Workforce Development Board and participate in strategy activities.  The Local 
Workforce Development Board understands that developing solid career pathways 
benefits the area in many ways.  The core partners will coordinate to transition 
participants into post-secondary education.   
 
Vocational Rehabilitation coordinates with Adult Education to provide transitional 
services.  Specialized vocational rehabilitation counselors are assigned to work with 
special education students as they prepare to leave high school.  Vocational 
Rehabilitation has also a transition specialist in the ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers to 
provide services to secondary and post- secondary students.   
i. Transportation.  A description of how the local board will coordinate workforce 
investment activities carried out under this title in the local area with the provision of 
transportation, including public transportation, and other appropriate supportive 
services in the local area. 
 
Transportation barriers continue to be an area of concern for low-income and 
individuals with barriers to employment in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties. 
 
Transportation is a huge barrier to employment in this large rural area comprised of the 
Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties spanning 25,973 square miles with only two public 
transportation systems, one in the city limits of Globe/Miami and the other within the 
city limits of Show Low, Pinetop, and Lakeside. The buses run limited hours therefore 
cannot serve those doing swing shift work or who must arrive at work prior to 7:00 AM 
or after 6:00 PM.  
 
Supportive services are provided to enable an individual to participant in Workforce 
Innovation and Opportunity Act services.  The Local Workforce Development Board has

<!-- page 442 -->
34 
 
the authority to approve the supportive services that it deems appropriate for eligible 
participants.    
 
Support services may be requested by any participant involved in Career Services for 
Adult and Dislocated Worker and Youth as one of the 14 program elements.  These 
services will be identified as part of the comprehensive assessments.  These 
assessments and all supportive services provided must by documented in the 
participant’s Individual Employment Plan (IEP) or Individual Service Strategy (ISS).   
 
Workforce Specialists for Title l provide the coordination of resources for support 
services both in the program and in the community when the supportive services are 
necessary to enable an individual who cannot afford to pay for such services, to 
participate in Workforce and Opportunity Act authorized services and they are not 
available from other sources.   
 
Supportive services will include: Transportation Assistance which includes Auto Repair, 
Gasoline, Training/Work Related Items, Medical - Health Related, Childcare, Utilities, 
Rent or Mortgage and Relocation Expenses.  All supportive services are based on the 
need of the participant to participate in Workforce activities.  
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has approved a Supportive Services policy that 
will cover transportation with gas cards for all Title I participants who demonstrate a 
need.  The Local Workforce Development Area provides transportation supportive 
services on a case-by-case basis if assistance is required to participate in an education or 
training activity and/or seeking employment.   
 
The difficulty in accessing transportation has been a driving force behind why it is 
necessary for the Local Workforce Development Board to have accessibility within each 
of the three counties: Navajo, Apache, and Gila.

<!-- page 443 -->
35 
 
Section 6:  Access 
 
A description of how the local board, working with the entities carrying out core 
programs, will expand access to employment, training, education, and supportive services 
for eligible individuals, 
a. Particularly eligible individuals with barriers to employment. 
Comprehensive and Affiliate ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers are located throughout Navajo, 
Apache, and Gila Counties.  These job centers are located in Globe, Payson, Winslow, Show 
Low, and Eagar and offer employers and job seekers access to information and coordinated 
services.  Each job center offers access to the ARIZONA@WORK services through staff co-
location and coordination/referral of services.  The job centers offer specialty trained staff 
and a variety of assistive technology to serve persons with disabilities. All Core Partners 
which include Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Programs, Adult Education, Employment 
Services, and Vocational Rehabilitation will continually collaborate and coordinate with 
each other to provide services to individuals with barriers to employment.  
 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers connect individuals to multiple partners to develop an 
employment plan for skill gaps and barriers to employment as defined by the partners.  The 
partners will collaborate within the employment plan to overcome these barriers to obtain 
unsubsidized employment.   
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has established a Priority of Services to ensure the 
delivery of career and training services for the Adult, Youth, Veteran, and eligible spouses of 
Veterans that are most in need.   
 
The Local Workforce Development Board will continue to pursue strategies to improve 
availability and coordination of ARIZONA@WORK System Services to employers and job 
seekers. This will enhance the Local Workforce Development Area’s ability to carry out the 
vision and goals for the workforce system: 
 
 Ensure that ALL jobseeker clients, regardless of where they first access the 
workforce system, will be registered in the ARIZONA@WORK database.  
 Establish initial screening questions to help in identifying potential partner 
engagement. 
 Establish partner lists of typical characteristics for each program in regard to 
potential eligibility and suitability for enrollment to assist with targeted referrals.     
 Partners will establish a referral process that kicks in based on the initial screening 
findings and has agreed that if the initial screening is done at the Job Centers, basic 
individualized services will be offered and provided. 
 Create a service map of available basic and individualized career services, training, 
and supportive services.

<!-- page 444 -->
36 
 
 Create a system orientation to be used at all access points in the Local Workforce 
Development Area. 
 Initiate a primary case management system where an individual maintains his or her 
case manager at the point of first enrollment to provide a single point of contact 
regardless of the subsequent funding streams he or she may be co-enrolled in.    
Subsequent funding streams will serve as a program contact that maintains 
appropriate recordkeeping to track progress and performance data. 
 Establish a tracking form that will be used until such time a statewide electronic 
tracking is available via an integrated case management system. 
 Core Program Partners will meet at least every two months to review referral 
listings, enrollments, co-enrollments, and exits.    These meetings will serve as 
informational to help partners follow through on referrals, to provide opportunities 
for pitching co-enrollment possibilities, discuss performance strategies, and to 
coordinate exits. 
 Core Program Partners that have employment as a planned outcome have agreed to 
participate on an integrated business service team including Adults, Dislocated 
Workers, Youth, Wagner-Peyser, and Vocational Rehabilitation. 
 Core Program Partners, Local Workforce Development Board, and Economic 
Development will assist in the support of sector relationships for the identified 
existing and emerging industries and occupations.   The sector relationships will 
incorporate career pathways into its responsibilities in terms of creating a flow of 
the career path options, analysis of the needs and expectations of business 
customers, analysis of the existing skills and knowledge of the talent pipeline, and 
work with education representatives to tap into existing training or create new 
credentialing or certification options.    
 Educational institutions have Carl Perkins options in the equation to assist with 
credits for eligible participants. 
 Core Program Partners have discussed and agreed upon the existing and emerging 
in-demand industries and occupations that the Local Workforce Development Area 
will focus on regarding business development and the building of a client talent 
pipeline to meet business needs and expectations. 
 
b. A description of how the local board will facilitate access to services provided through 
the one-stop delivery system, including in remote areas, through the use of 
technology and through other means. 
 
Access to services will be achieved through a variety of mechanisms starting with 
training all core program partners on registering individuals in the Arizona Job 
Connection database, providing workforce system initial screening questions, and 
offering the system-wide orientation that is being developed.   This “no wrong door” 
concept allows multiple and varied locations.  
     
Technology is encouraged throughout the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act.   
Information will be provided on all core program partner websites on services that are

<!-- page 445 -->
37 
 
available via the web.    Information on how to access web resources will be provided via 
the system orientation and through outreach and recruitment materials. 
 
Social media will continue to play an increasing role in the delivery of services, 
particularly to youth participants and other clients.  The use of Facebook text messaging 
and Twitter increases case management services and provides connectivity in this day 
and age.  There will be expanded use of technical sites for obtaining participant 
information such as educational and employment outcome, which includes Arizona Job 
Connection, DES Guide, and the work number.  
 
Technology will be strengthened in job seekers through the use of on-line assessments 
such as TABE and GED.  Also on-line assessments for screening applicants as employers 
utilize customized assessments and more standardized industry recognized tools such as 
Workkeys.     
 
Due to the rural nature of the Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties, case managers can 
travel to remote areas to deliver services face to face when the situation warrants. 
c. A description of how entities within the one-stop delivery system, including one-stop 
operators and the ARIZONA@WORK partners, will comply with section 188, if 
applicable, and applicable provisions of the Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990 (42 
U.S.C. 12101 et seq.) regarding the physical and programmatic accessibility of 
facilities, programs and services, technology, and materials for individuals with 
disabilities, including providing staff training and support for addressing the needs of 
individuals with disabilities. 
The Local Workforce Development Board makes every effort to ensure that staff can 
and does accommodate persons with disabilities effectively.   
 
The Local Workforce Development Board supports and requires compliance as follows: 
 
 Section 188 of the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act prohibiting 
discrimination on the grounds of race, color, religion, sex, national origin, age, 
disability, political affiliation or belief.    
 Section 188 of the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act requires reasonable 
accommodations be provided to qualified individuals with disabilities in certain 
circumstances.     
All Local Workforce Development Area partners will post required notices to ensure all 
eligible individuals are aware of the system’s obligations to operate programs in a non-
discriminatory manner.  Equal Employment Opportunity is the Law notices will be 
posted in all partner facilities and will be reviewed with each program participant and 
documented in each client file.  Equal Employment Opportunity language will be placed 
on all new and reprinted outreach and recruitment documents.

<!-- page 446 -->
38 
 
All ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers will be accessible to individuals with disabilities 
including the mobile unit.     
EO Monitoring will include a review of EO system compliance regarding all of the above.  
The Local Workforce Development Board is requesting that the Arizona Department of 
Economic Security - Rehabilitation Services do a review of the ARIZONA@WORK Job 
Centers, as part of their in-kind resource contribution, to validate compliance.  In 
addition, Rehabilitation Services is providing training to the Local Workforce 
Development Area partner staff regarding serving individuals with disabilities as part of 
the Resource Sharing Agreement. 
i. Describe a system that includes compliance with Workforce Innovation and 
Opportunity Act (WIOA) Section 188, Title IV of Civil Rights Act of 1964, Section 
504 of Rehabilitation Act of 1973, Age Discrimination Act of 1975 and Title IX of 
the Education Act of 1972, and the current State of Arizona Method of 
Administration (MOA). 
In addition, to Section 188 of the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act, all 
Local Workforce Development partners are required to comply with: 
 
 Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act, which prohibits discrimination against 
individuals with disabilities by recipients of Federal financial assistance. 
 Title I and Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act, which prohibits 
discrimination in employment based on a disability, and prohibits State and local 
governments from discriminating on the basis of disability. 
 Section 427 of the General Education Provisions Act that requires recipients 
ensure equitable access to, and participation in, certain programs run by the 
United States Department of Education. 
 State of Arizona Nondiscrimination Plan. 
ii. Reference policies in process. 
As additional guidance is provided by the State for the Workforce Innovation and 
Opportunity Act, the Local Workforce Development Board has written Equal 
Opportunity Compliant policies and procedures. 
iii. Describe how each access site identified in the plan will ensure compliance with all 
elements in the State’s MOA (i.e., Equal Opportunity and Americans with 
Disabilities Act requirements, including meeting LEP communication 
requirements). 
Compliance with states Nondiscrimination Plan 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area has developed and maintains a reasonable 
accommodation policy. The ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers make every effort to 
ensure that staff can and do accommodate persons with disabilities effectively.

<!-- page 447 -->
39 
 
Communication tools are provided to more easily serve these individuals. The 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers are equal opportunity employers and providers of 
employment and training services. Auxiliary aids and services are available upon 
request to persons with disabilities. 
 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Center partners participant in the Workplace Accessibility 
Training and Assistive Technology seminars each quarter to better understand the 
Americans with Disability Act.   
 
The Local Workforce Development Board adopted an Equal Opportunity and 
Nondiscrimination Policy, WIA Administration Procedure #2010-002 Equal 
Opportunity and Nondiscrimination Policy.  The Local Workforce Development Area 
will comply with the State’s Nondiscrimination Plan in each of the ARIZONA@WORK 
Job Centers. 
 
The State’s Nondiscrimination Plan is comprised of nine elements that describe the 
actions the Local Workforce Development Board has taken and will continue to take 
to ensure that its WIOA Title 1-financially assisted programs, activities, and 
recipients are complying, and will continue to comply, with the nondiscrimination 
and equal opportunity requirements of WIOA and regulations. 
 
The LWDB is reviewing the following policies for implementation to ensure 
compliance with non-discrimination: 
Designation of Local Level Equal Opportunity Officer: The Equal Opportunity 
Officers was designated by the Local Workforce Development Board.  Notification of 
the EO Officers’ identities are posted on the “Equal Opportunity is the Law” posters 
in the lobby and ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers in both English and Spanish plus a 
copy is given to each WIOA participant.  This signed copy is place in the participants 
file. 
 
Notice and Communication: The Local Workforce Development Board has 
established a notice and communication system that is accessible to all registrants, 
applicants, eligible applicants/registrants, applicants for employment, employees 
and interested members of the public, making them aware of their obligation to 
operate programs and activities in a nondiscriminatory manner, and the extent of 
the rights of members of these protected groups to file complaints of 
discrimination. 
 
Applicants, participants, other recipients, members of the public and individuals 
with disabilities are notified of the procedures to request accommodations. 
Accommodations are available upon requests in all locations.

<!-- page 448 -->
40 
 
Review Assurances, Job Training Plans, Contract and Policies and Procedures: The 
Local Workforce Development Board has incorporated the language required for 
Equal Opportunity (EO) and Affirmative Action into all its contracts and agreements 
and provides assurance for compliance in the yearly Arizona WIOA Equal 
Opportunity and Nondiscrimination Technical Assistance Monitoring Guide. Training 
providers, service providers and contractors are required to certify that they can 
provide programmatic and architectural accessibility for individuals with disabilities 
and will comply with EO policies. 
 
Policies on WIOA Title I nondiscrimination and/or equal opportunity issues are 
developed and implemented by the Local Workforce Development Board. 
 
Universal Access: ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers have made efforts to broaden 
participation of individuals with disabilities in the WIOA-funded programs and 
activities. Press releases showcasing accomplishments for clients from all current 
programs have been published. Public service announcements on local radio 
stations and newspapers for available program services have been produced.  
 
WIOA brochures and public materials include photos of women and persons of 
all ages and races. WIOA Program photos capture the racially and ethnically 
diverse culture of persons seeking service in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area has a Local Area EO Officer who conducts 
on-site quality assurance on a bi-annual basis of the Comprehensive, Affiliate 
ARIZONA@WORK Job Centers, and sub-grantees to ensure that equitable and all 
non-discrimination policies (Affirmative Action, Americans with Disabilities Act, and 
Equal Opportunity Employment) are adhered to as required by law and regulation.   
No person shall be discriminated against on the grounds of age, gender, disability 
religion, race, color, national origin, citizenship and participation. The following 
requirements are taken from regulation 29 CFR Part 37 and must be incorporated 
into the system and practices of all recipients for assurances of nondiscrimination. 
Any program and activity that is federally funded under WIOA Title 1 is a recipient 
and are subject to these requirements. 
 
Affirmative Action Policy 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board’s Affirmative Action Policy is in compliance 
with the WIOA  Section 188(a)(1),(2),(4), and (5) which ensures that no individual 
shall be excluded from participation in, denied benefit of, subjected to 
discrimination under, or denied employment in the administration of, or in 
connection with any such program under the title. The EO Officer monitors files on a 
quarterly basis to ensure that the Local Workforce Development Area services 
adhere to the Affirmative Action Policy.

<!-- page 449 -->
41 
 
Americans with Disabilities Act 
The Local Workforce Development Board is in compliance with the Americans with 
Disabilities Act of 1990, all requirements imposed by or pursuant to the 
implementing regulations. The Local Workforce Development Board works in 
collaboration with partner programs such as: Vocational Rehabilitation, Employment 
Service, Adult Education, and Veterans to ensure compliance for Americans with 
disabilities to ensure compliance. 
iv. Provide contact information and identification of the local, state, and federal EO 
Officers.   Explain how it’s available in all facilities that are used to conduct WIOA 
Title I funded activities or trainings. 
 
 
 
 
EO Is the Law flyers are posted in the Comprehensive and Affiliate ARIZONA@WORK 
Job Centers in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties. 
v.   discrimination complaint process that ensures that no individual is excluded from 
participation in or with a WIOA Title I funded activity, training or employment for 
any reason. 
Equal Opportunity Employment Policy 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has an Equal Opportunity Employment 
Policy in compliance with Section 188(a)(1) and (2) which ensures that no individual 
shall be subjected to discrimination relating to employment in the administration of 
or in connection with any such program under this title. The LWDB works closely 
with its various partners to ensure that services are delivered on a non-

discriminatory basis. 
 
If a complaint is filed with the State of Arizona, the individual must wait either until 
the State of Arizona issues a written Notice of Final Action, or until 90 days have 
passed, (whichever is sooner), before filing a complaint with the Civil Rights Center.    
If the State of Arizona does not give the individual a written Notice of Final Action 
within 90 days of the day on which the complaint was file, the individual does not 
have to wait for the State of Arizona to issue that Notice before filing a complaint 
with the Civil Rights Center.   However, the individual must file the Civil Rights 
Complaint within 30 days of the 90-day deadline (in other words, within 120 days 
after the day on which the complaint was filed with the State of Arizona).   If the 
State of Arizona gives the individual a written Notice of Final Action on the 
complaint, but the individual is dissatisfied with the decision or resolution, the 
individual may file a complaint with the Civil Rights Center.    The individual must file 
the Civil Rights Center complaint within 30 days of the date on which the individual 
received the Notice of Final Action. 
 
All complaints will be logged and timeframes documented to ensure compliance 
with the law, regulations, and policies.

<!-- page 451 -->
43 
 
Section 7:  Employer Engagement/Coordination with 
Economic Development 
 Provide a description of how the local board/local area will: 
a. Coordinate workforce investment activities carried out in the local area with economic 
development activities carried out in the region in which the local area is located. 
Economic Development has participated in the Local Workforce Development Board to 
discuss and formulate strategies to serve business and industry effectively and played an 
important advisory role in the development of the local plan including input regarding 
existing and emerging industries and occupations that are forecasted to be in-demand 
during the upcoming four years.     
 
The Local Workforce Development Board and Economic Development have agreed to 
participate in collecting information from business customers regarding the skills and 
knowledge they are looking for to fill entry level and middle skill jobs in the in-demand 
occupations.   
 
Navajo County Economic Development will provide a training session to the Local 
Workforce Development Board and partners on what they do and how the workforce 
system and economic development can work together.  They also committed to 
assisting the workforce system with identifying job-shadowing opportunities for 
jobseekers.  
b. Focus on sector strategies (include a description); include statewide sectors that exist 
in the local area plus local area specific sectors. 
Core Program Partners, Gila Community College, Northland Pioneer College, and 
Economic Development will assist in the support of sector relationships for the 
identified existing and emerging industries and occupations.    
 
The sector relationships will incorporate career pathways into its responsibilities in 
terms of creating a flow of the career path options, analysis of the needs and 
expectations of business customers, analysis of the existing skills and knowledge of the 
talent pipeline, and work with education representatives to tap into existing training or 
create new credentialing or certification options.    
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has identified Healthcare, Construction, 
Utilities, Manufacturing, and Transportation sectors and associated career pathways 
in Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties.  In partnership with Local Workforce 
Development Board Members, Employers, and Community Colleges, workgroups will 
be formed to focus on the workforce needs within each sector.  The Local Workforce 
Development Board will take a lead role in organizing the sector workgroups.

<!-- page 452 -->
44 
 
The Local Workforce Board has targeted two areas that are aligned with the state’s 
selected industry sectors: Advanced Manufacturing and Health Care. These areas are 
compatible with the Local Workforce Development Board’s existing and emerging 
industries and growth occupations.  
c. Facilitate engagement of employers, including small employers and employers in in-
demand industry sectors and occupations, in workforce development programs. 
The Local Workforce Development Board will continue its outreach and relationship- 
building with local businesses through partnership with Economic Development, 
Chambers of Commerce, and industry associations.  Business customers play a key role 
in the success of the Workforce Development System. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area  partners will make concerted efforts to identify, 
understand and respond to the specific needs of its current and future employers. We 
will: 
 
 Outreach to small and large employers and In-Demand industries 
 Targeted visits to various community meetings, associations, chambers 
 Outreach to colleges 
 Design training in concert with Community Colleges for the purposes of career 
pathways that provide skilled workers based on employer need. 
 
d. Support a local workforce development system that meets the needs of businesses in 
the local area. 
Employer needs and expectations will be established through the work of our sector 
relationships along with recommended career pathways flow for each industry and 
occupation.   Economic development partners in the Local Workforce Development Area 
have agreed to assist with the sector relationships and gather information on the needs 
and expectations of business and industry.   Existing and emerging industries and 
occupations representatives who are Local Workforce Development Board Members 
will participate on the appropriate sector discussions along with union representatives 
and educational institutions.   
   
Successful workforce systems utilize both job development and business development 
methods in serving business and industry customers and assisting jobseekers in getting 
and keeping employment.  It is understood that job development and business 
development are very different outreach and recruitment approaches to working with 
business and industry.     
 
The Local Workforce Development Board sees job development as an approach that 
requires specific funding sources to reach out to small and family-owned businesses 
with a particular client in mind in an effort to place the individual with known barriers,

<!-- page 453 -->
45 
 
strengths, and weaknesses in a work-based learning opportunity.    Job development will 
remain the responsibility and role of individual partner programs.   
 
Business development, on the other hand, is about outreach and recruitment of 
business customers to request applicants when openings occur.   Typically, business 
development is medium size to large businesses.   The Local Workforce Development 
Board will form an integrated Business Service Team to conduct business development.    
    
As members of the Integrated Business Service Team, Local Workforce Development 
Board members, core program partners as well as Economic Development, Gila 
Community College, and Northland Pioneer College will align and integrate business 
development activities within the Local Development Area for the purpose of: 
 
 Creating a single point of contact for businesses 
 Establishing a collaborative outreach and recruitment structure  
 Coordinating workforce services and training options 
 Leveraging resources  
 Sharing performance outcomes 
 Creating and managing a talent pipeline in existing and emerging in-demand 
industries and occupations 
 Establishing an inventory of existing training options, particularly those that include 
a credential or certification in existing and emerging in-demand industries and 
occupations 
 Identifying skill and knowledge gaps between what business needs and expects and 
what training options are available 
 Working with Education to enhance or establish short-term training options for 
portable or stackable credential/certification opportunities 
 Working with Economic Development Representatives to ensure a dynamic 
approach to meeting the ever-changing needs of business and industry as the in-
demand sector landscape grows or diminishes 
 
e. That may include the implementation of initiatives such as incumbent worker training 
programs, on-the-job training programs, customized training programs, industry and 
sector strategies, career pathways initiatives, utilization of effective business 
intermediaries, and other business services and strategies, designed to meet the 
needs of employers in the corresponding region in support of the strategy described in 
(1)(F). 
f. Strengthen linkages between the one-stop delivery system and unemployment 
insurance programs. 
The Comprehensive ARIZONA@WORK Job Center staff assists Unemployment 
Insurance claimants in creating individual reemployment plans based on current labor 
market information. Unemployment Insurance Claimants are required to register in

<!-- page 454 -->
46 
 
the Arizona Job Connection database. The staff ensures that claimants are engaging in 
regular job search efforts to obtain work.  Staff provides a series of job search 
workshops that are designed to facilitate the job seeker’s success. 
 
 
Comprehensive and collaborative services to job seekers through the ARIZONA@WORK 
Job Center System include referrals, job applications, and assistance job openings, 
training and education programs. The claimants who are most likely to exhaust 
benefits before finding employment are targeted for more skills development.

<!-- page 455 -->
47 
 
Section 8:  Program and Service Delivery 
A description of how the local board and partners will make available programs and 
services. 
a. A description and assessment of the type and availability of adult and dislocated 
worker employment and training activities in the local area. 
 
 The Local Workforce Development Board wants to make available as many options for 
Individualized Career Services and Training as possible to allow a tailored unique 
experience based on client needs.    This supports our customer-center design.   The 
local workforce system will not prescribe to “a one size fits all” mentality.    The toolbox 
of opportunities will be extensive providing front-line staff and integrated business 
service team members a chance to individualize the workforce system experience and 
create a meaningful plan of action.     
 The Adult Program provides assistance for low income individuals ages 18 and older, 
with significant barriers to employment.  It provides priority of services to veterans, 
public assistance recipients, and other low income individuals who may be basic skills 
deficient.   
 The Dislocated Worker Program provides services to individuals who have been laid off, 
or receiving a notice of layoff through no fault of their own from employment generally 
due to closures or downsizing, self-employment individuals who are unemployed due to 
general economic conditions and displaced homemaker who have been dependent on a 
family members income and that income is no longer available may also be eligible for 
services. 
The assessment of the type and availability of these employment and training activities 
was conducted through a service mapping exercise performed by the partners.  In some 
instances, the services are Basic Career Services that one or more partner agencies may 
be assisting with.   In other instances, such as Adult Education and Literacy, a Core 
Program Partner will provide the service.   Adults and Dislocated Workers may be co-
enrolled across partner programs to leverage resources and expand the menu options.      
Self-Service and Basic Career Services are provided to all job seekers. Triage and initial 
assessment will be offered by members of the Welcome Team in the ARIZONA@WORK 
Job Center and Affiliate Job Centers to determine whether the customer is in crisis, 
needs assistance with basic needs, or is ready to work and/or get on a path toward a 
career.  The Welcome Team will be cross-trained on services offered by core and 
partner programs to make appropriate referrals for the customer.  To serve all their 
needs customers may also be referred to community service resources for assistant.  
Other provision of information includes guidance with filing unemployment insurance 
claims.

<!-- page 456 -->
48 
 
These services may be independent or staff assisted.  Customers have access to 
computers with internet, phones, fax machines, and copier, as well as a list of current 
job postings and job search web sites in the area.  Customers do job search and may 
receive placement assistance and career counseling, development of a Career Plan and 
labor market information.  Service delivery includes use of the Arizona Job Connection 
internet based site and other job databases sites, DES website, plus access to career 
research websites and materials. 
Also staff can provide assistance and support customers with up-to-date knowledge on 
area job openings, employer information and the wide array of partner services 
available.  They will guide individuals to websites that are the most useful, offer advice 
regarding effective and engaging resume and cover letter templates and provide any 
other assistance that they can.  Staff will guide job seekers to WIOA programs.  This 
includes information on eligibility and individual career service activities, such as 
supportive services, work experience, on the job training, and occupational skills 
training.  Workshops are conducted on a regular scheduled bases on Resume, Interview 
techniques, and completing job applications. 
Individual Career Services are offered to all Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act 
participants who are not able to obtain employment by self-directed or Basic Career 
Services, or who have barriers to employment not able to be addressed in other ways.  
They may also be enrolled into the Individual Career Services.  This includes the 
development of an individual employment plan (IEP), case management, and one-on-
one career coaching, structured job search, career planning, and research on training 
options and soft skills, and supportive services. 
  The Local Workforce Development Board recognizes the need for supportive services 
necessary for individuals to participant in Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act 
activities.  Supportive services must be determined necessary to transition to self-
sufficiency including completion of training and/or job placement.  They include but are 
not limited to: transportation, training or work related tools and supplies, health care, 
clothing, utilities.   The case manager and participant review the possibility of obtaining 
the needed supportive services through other programs/community resources providing 
such assistance.  This will ensure resources and services coordination in the local area 
and encourage a referral system.  
Training Services are available in many forms, dependent on circumstances and desired 
outcomes.  Listed below are the descriptions 
Occupational Skills Training is provided through an Individual Training Account Voucher 
(ITA).  These are offered to participants who do not possess the occupational skills 
necessary to obtain employment in the local area, or who have not found employment 
through basic and individual career services due to substantial barriers to employment 
and self-sufficiency. When developing the training plan, the case manager and 
participant will make sure the training is suitable for the participant by using the O’Net

<!-- page 457 -->
49 
 
on-line assessment and analyzing the result.  They will review the labor market 
information and the area of Occupations In-Demand list to be certain the training will 
lead to an In-Demand Occupation job.  Also the training plan might include the 
supportive services needs for the participant to be successful in the training.  Once all 
this is in place, the ITA for occupational training will be completed and submitted to the 
training facility.     
  On-the-Job Training (OJT) can be offered to employers to provide financial assistance in 
order that new employers can learn basic skills they need to perform their job.  On-the-
Job Training (OJT) can be offered to employers who meet requirements outlined in the 
local OJT Policies.  
This training can be offered to participants who are eligible for enrollment in the 
WIOA Programs. The training will be designed to provide knowledge or skills essential 
to the full and adequate performance of the job. A complete list of the 
skills/knowledge to be mastered and the timeline to acquire them shall be included in 
the OJT contract negotiated with the employer.  
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has approved for an OJT to be reimbursed 
up to 50% of their wages while being trained in an occupation.  The OJT Employer 
Agreement outlines the duration of the training, an outline of the job description, and 
job skills to be obtained by the end of the agreement. 
 
Employment Work-Related activities are planned, structured learning experiences that 
take place in a workplace for a limited period of time.  They are subject to labor 
standards that apply where an employee and employer relationship as defined in the 
Fair Labor Standards Act.   A training stipend is negotiated and paid every two weeks. 
The Local Workforce Development Board is responsible for the needs of participants, 
recruits sound work sites that are willing to train, provides support and guidance to the 
employer, and conducts progress reports every two weeks to ensure goals are achieved. 
The Local Workforce Development Board recognizes that Apprenticeship is a valuable 
training tool for many in-demand occupations.  All possible opportunities to develop 
new apprenticeships will be pursued and supported.  Currently the limited availability of 
training in this area makes it difficult for many participants to take advantage of this 
opportunity.   
In the Globe Comprehensive ARIZONA@WORK Job Center, the Adult, Dislocated 
Worker, and Youth Program case manager provides a service to the Electrical 
Apprenticeship Program by pre-screening their applicants with administering the Test 
for Adult Basic Education (TABE) assessment.

<!-- page 458 -->
50 
 
b.   A description of how the local board will coordinate workforce investment activities 
carried out in the local area with statewide rapid response activities as described in 
section 134(a)(2)(A). 
 
Rapid Response provides services to assist employers, employees and communities 
facing a plant closure, or a substantial layoff due to economic slowdown or foreign 
competition. Rapid Response is a State responsibility.  The State contracts with the Local 
Workforce Development Board to provide rapid response services 
 
Rapid Response will play a major role in the success of the three rural counties: Navajo, 
Apache and Gila County. Rapid Response services are delivered through a team made up 
of Title I Dislocated Worker Program, Employment Service, and Unemployment 
Insurance, Trade Adjustment Act, and Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act 
partners as determined.  Title I Dislocated Worker Program is the lead agency and 
makes initial contact with the employer to initiate the process within 48 hours.  
 
Through coordination developed between Title I Dislocated Worker Program, 
Employment Service, Unemployment Insurance and other local agencies. The rapid 
response team leader will respond to employers and the benefits of providing their 
affected workers with informational orientations, define the employer's role prior to 
provision of services, identify services required and the number to be served. 
 
Rapid Response Team Leader will meet with local agencies to inform them of the scope 
of layoff or closure and discuss the services to be provided.  The team will identify local 
organizations that can provide assistance in the informational orientation.  The team 
will identify agencies that will participate in the information orientations, identify their 
role, establish an agenda, identify the date(s) for the informational orientation and 
confirm with the affected employer.   
 
The Rapid Response Team coordinates with Employment Service, Veterans Services, 
Unemployment Service, and other WIOA partners.  Depending on the nature of the 
layoff and requests from employers, other agency representatives may participate in 
the Rapid Response Team including Trade Adjustment Act staff; union representatives, 
or other social services agencies.   
 
There are two Rapid Response Coordinators that will respond to the employer and 
organize the rapid response orientations.  One coordinates Navajo and Apache County 
and the other coordinates Gila County. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has designated seven (7) employees at one 
employer as the approved minimum threshold number of affected workers for Rapid 
Response services to be activated.

<!-- page 459 -->
51 
 
The state will notify the rapid response team leader if they receive a Worker Adjustment 
and Retraining Notification (WARN) indicating a mass layoff of 50 or more affected 
workers. Company data is entered on www.azjobconnection.gov under the Worker 
Adjustment and Retraining Notification (WARN) section to assist in statewide tracking, 
and to alert other areas of the layoff. This system allows information sharing statewide 
so all areas may be aware of Dislocated Workers seeking new employment.  In addition 
to WARN Notices, employers that do not have to release a WARN Notice will be treated 
the same as those that do as long as they meet the minimum threshold number of 
affected workers.   
 
Rapid Response Team will conduct the Rapid Response Orientation. Rapid Response 
Team will present the ARIZONA@WORK Service System which includes the Dislocated 
Worker Program: 
 
Rapid Response team will present the information on services offered through 
Dislocated Worker Program, Employment Service and other programs which will include 
the following services: 
  
 Labor Market Information 
 Basic Education Skills 
 Occupational Skills Training 
 On-the-Job Training  
 Career Planning 
 Individual Employment Plan 
 Job Search and Job Referral  
 Job Skills Workshops  
 Referrals to other agencies 
 Resume Preparation   
 Assistance with Job Applications 
 
Averting layoffs is one of the functions of Rapid Response.   Layoff Aversion is defined 
as a worker’s job is saved with an existing employer that is at risk of downsizing or 
closure; or a worker at risk of dislocation who transitions to a different job with the 
same employer, or a new job with a different employer and experiences no or a 
minimal spell of unemployment.    
 
The Local Workforce Development Business Service Team will work with the local 
Chambers of Commerce and community-based organizations to identify at-risk 
companies.   The Business Service Team will establish guidelines for assessing at-risk 
companies including such indicators as declining sales, supply chain issues, adverse 
industry market trends, and changes in management or ownership.

<!-- page 460 -->
52 
 
c. A description and assessment of the type and availability of youth workforce 
investment activities in the local area, including activities for youth who are 
individuals with disabilities, which description and assessment shall include an 
identification of successful models of such youth workforce investment activities.  
Please indicate which youth services are provided through competitively secured 
providers, sole sourcing, or other means.   Describe the process for selecting youth 
providers on a competitive basis, including criteria used to identify youth providers 
(see WIOA section 123, draft regulations 681.400, and the WIOA State Plan section 
(VI.c.) 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area provides all of the program elements services 
in-house that are allowed by WIOA legislation, regulations and waivers approved for the 
State of Arizona.  The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act requires that a 
minimum of 75% of the Title I youth funds, minus administrative costs, must be spent 
on out-of- school youth.  The Local Workforce Development Board has chosen to use its 
Title I funds for out-of-school youth services ages 16-24 and in-school youth services. 
 
Also, the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act requires a minimum of 20% of the 
Local Workforce Development Area Title I youth funds, minus administrative costs, must 
be spent on work based training. 
 Academic and Occupational Education 
 Summer Jobs 
 Pre-Apprenticeship Programs 
 On-the-Job Training 
 Job Shadowing and internships 
Program expenditures on the work experience program element included wages as well 
as staffing costs for the development and management of the work experiences. 
Examples of the 20% priority spending on work experiences: 
 Youth Wages including Federal Insurance Contributions Act (FICA tax 
 Staffing and travel costs for developing and providing work readiness training for 
youth participants 
 Staffing and travel costs to meet and work with employers to develop work 
experiences for youth participants 
 Staffing and travel costs for on-site monitoring and job coaching at the youth 
participants’ work site 
The Local Workforce Development Area Title I Youth Program will include the fourteen 
(14) required program elements in our menu of services. 
 
The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act requires that all 14 (fourteen) program 
elements are “available” to all youth who are served by the Workforce Innovation and

youth who lack the family, educational, and social frameworks to meet their essential

participant, based on each participant’s objective assessment and individual service

<!-- page 464 -->
56 
 
As with the Adult and Dislocated Worker Programs, all fourteen (14) elements will not 
necessarily be provided by the Title I Youth service provider.  Youth may receive some 
services through coordination with Core Partner Programs including Adult Education 
and Literacy, Wagner Peyser, Vocational Rehabilitation, or other Partner Programs.  
When appropriate, youth will be co-enrolled in partner programs. 
 
The local youth programs are designed to prepare our youth to enter post-secondary 
education, training or employment upon completion of their secondary education.  The 
main purpose of this initiative is to increase the focus on longer-term academic and 
occupational learning opportunities and provide long term comprehensive service 
strategies.  The youth services provides an opportunity for disconnected youth to 
reconnect through multiple career pathways to education and training opportunities 
necessary to enter and advance in the working world.  Youth services will target out of 
school youth 16-24.   
 
Outreach and Recruitment, Intake and Eligibility Determination, Objective Assessment, 
Development of Individual Service Strategy, Case Management, and Follow Up Services 
are essential for a youth to succeed. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area gives service priority for out of school youth 
who are considered most at-risk, which can be defined as high school dropouts, 
runaway and homeless youth, youth in foster care, court-involved youth, children of 
incarcerated parents, youth who are basic skills deficient, and pregnant and parenting 
youth. 
 
According to the 2015 Dropout Rate Study Report by County from Arizona Department 
of Education, Apache County had a 4.5% dropout rate, Gila a 5.1% dropout rate, and 
Navajo County a 5.1% dropout rate compared to the Arizona state rate of 3.46%.  
Arizona has one of the worst high school dropout rates in the nation. 
 
In March of 2016 Arizona Department of Health released information on Teen 
Pregnancy from 2004-2014.  According to this report for the year 2014, of the 445,214 
teen pregnancies in youth ages 19 and under, 5,975 were in Apache County, 2,968 in 
Gila County, and 8,224 were in Navajo county attributing a total of 17,167 or 3.9% to the 
Local Workforce Development Area. 
 
Outreach and recruitment is conducted through referrals from partner programs and 
presentations to programs who work with youth in the local area, including juvenile 
probation, Arizona Youth Partnership, Women/Infant/Children (WIC) Program, and Teen 
Outreach Pregnancy Services. 
 
Once a youth is identified, they are provided with a program orientation and local staff 
determines whether the youth qualifies as an out of school youth.  At this time, an 
objective assessment will take place.  The objective assessment, or, intake process will

<!-- page 465 -->
57 
 
include a review of which of the fourteen program elements may be needed by the 
youth.  An Individual Service Strategy will be developed between the Workforce 
Specialist and youth participant that may require some testing and assessment to 
determine what services are needed for the youth.  The Individual Service Strategy tells 
the story of where the youth has been, where he or she is now, where the youth wants 
and needs to go, and how he or she will get there. 
 
The program staff will utilize the Individual Service Strategy to case manage the 
individual, personally assisting them with achieving their educational and employment 
goals.  Once the participant has achieved these goals, they will be exited from the 
program and twelve months of follow up services will be provided to ensure the youths’ 
success in the workforce. 
 
d. A description of how training services under chapter 3 of subtitle B will be provided in 
accordance with section 134(c)(3)(G), including, if contracts for the training services 
will be used, how the use of such contracts will be coordinated with the use of 
individual training accounts under that chapter and how the local board will ensure 
informed customer choice in the selection of training programs regardless of how the 
training services are to be provided. 
 
Training services for the Local Workforce Development Area will be provided in 
accordance with 134(c)(3)(G) of the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act.    
Training services provided will maximize customer choice in the selection of an eligible 
provider of such services.  
 
As required in the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act, in the Local Workforce 
Development Area, training services shall be directly linked to an in-demand industry 
sector or occupation or related entry level career path course of study.    Input from 
economic development, employers, core program partners, and labor market 
information resulted in the Local Workforce Development Board focusing on new and 
emerging industries and occupations including health care, construction, utilities, 
manufacturing, and transportation. 
 
Labor market data and local economic conditions can change with little warning.   As a 
result, the Local Workforce Development Board may decide to approve training services 
for occupations determined by the Board to be in sectors of the economy that have a 
high potential for sustained demand or growth in the Local Workforce Development 
Area.  If this occurs, Local Workforce Development Board will document the decision in 
Board meeting minutes along with the justification for the decision. 
 
Training services will be done through the Individual Training Account process for 
educational institution training services and through a contract process for other 
training services including on-the-job training, customized training, incumbent worker

<!-- page 466 -->
58 
 
training, or transitional employment.  The Local Workforce Development Board will not 
directly provide training services. 
 
Training services for Occupational Skills Training will be listed on the State Eligible 
Training Provider List approved by the Local Workforce Development Board. 
 
Customer choice can be achieved through a comprehensive case management strategy 
that involves an assessment and the development of an Individual Employment Plan 
that leads to training and self-sufficiency.   The Case Manager’s role is to assist the 
participant with making an informed choice after looking at the Eligible Training 
Provider List, program of study, length of training, and cost information, and taking into 
considerations the dollar amount of the training and the Board policy on expenditures. 
Once the training plan is in place, the Individual Training Account Voucher will be 
submitted to the training provider. 
 
e. How the local board will facilitate co-enrollment, as appropriate, in core programs. 
 
The core partners have agreed upon some fundamental concepts to achieve alignment 
and integration of service delivery including: 
 
The core program partners will work together to streamline connections to all programs 
a participant can benefit from by streamlining connections to programs through 
orientations that include information about all partner programs, referrals between 
partner programs when appropriate, communication between partner program case 
managers and participants, designing a common participant release of information 
honored by all partner programs, selecting a lead case manager within the partner 
programs to coordinate communication, and ensuring there is no duplication of services 
between programs. 
 
Core program partners will meet bi-monthly to review referral listings, enrollments, co-
enrollments, and exits.    These meetings will serve as informational to help partners 
follow through on referrals, to provide opportunities for pitching co-enrollment 
possibilities, discuss performance strategies, and to coordinate exits. 
 
f. A description of how the local area will promote entrepreneurial skills training and 
microenterprise services. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Area will work with local community-based 
organizations and economic development to co-sponsor entrepreneurial skills training 
opportunities.  In addition, Workforce staff will provide resources for participants who 
are looking to start a business such as contact information for local economic 
development and Arizona Office of Economic Opportunity.

<!-- page 467 -->
59 
 
Workforce staff will provide information to local microenterprises regarding available 
incentives and tax credits, as well as information about On-the-Job Training.   
 
g. How the local board will facilitate the development of career pathways. 
 
Core Program Partners, the Local Workforce Development Board, Gila Community 
College, Northland Pioneer College, and Economic Development Representatives will 
assist in the support of sector relationships for the identified existing and emerging 
industries and occupations.   The sector relationships will incorporate career pathways 
into its responsibilities in terms of creating a flow of the career path options, analysis of 
the needs and expectations of business customers, analysis of the existing skills and 
knowledge of the talent pipeline.  The Local Workforce Development Board will work 
with education representatives to tap into existing training programs or create new and 
short-term certification options to provide a skilled workforce to business customers.  
 
Skill gaps, such as basic skills and soft skills and barriers to employment will be 
addressed with the core partners to also provide skilled workforce to business 
customers.  Core Partners will coordinate to enable individuals to succeed in their career 
pathways by identifying barriers and assisting the individuals to overcome such barriers. 
 
h. How the local board will improve access to activities leading to a recognized 
postsecondary credential (including a credential that is an industry-recognized 
certificate or certification, portable, and stackable) 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board will continue to communicate with local 
businesses and economic development to remain focused on occupations most In-
Demand in the local area.  This collection of information is vital to ensure that a pipeline 
of skilled workers is developed.  These demand occupations will then be communicated 
to local community colleges to develop curriculum that aligns with occupations In-
Demand.  Workforce Specialists will work with customers to align them with career 
pathways that address these needs and will funnel them through proper partner 
programs to ensure their success, addressing barriers along the way. 
 
Skill gaps, such as basic skills will be addressed with the core partners to also provide 
assistance to individuals developing their career pathway.   Core partners will 
coordinate to enable individuals to succeed in their career pathways that lead to a 
credential by identifying barriers and assisting the individuals to overcome such barriers.  
 
The Local Workforce Development Board has included occupational skill training in the 
menu of services, which may include priority consideration for training programs that 
lead to recognized post-secondary credentials that are aligned with in-demand industry 
sectors or occupations.

<!-- page 468 -->
60 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board is working with apprenticeship training to 
provide opportunity to participants to obtain apprenticeship training which will lead to 
placement in employment, as well as opportunity to advance credentials. 
 
Access will be greatly improved with the coordination and integration of basic career 
services among the core partners.    A focus on meeting the needs and expectations of 
business and industry through the development of a talent pipeline will result in a 
variety of credentialing methods including portable and stackable credentials, as well as 
industry-recognized credentials and certifications that may be on-site with a business, or 
completed through an educational setting and/or Apprenticeship opportunity.

<!-- page 469 -->
61

performance of the local fiscal agent (where appropriate), eligible providers under 
subtitle B, and the one-stop delivery system, in the local area.

Progress toward the measures is distributed by the state monthly in the state’s Red and 
Green Report.  Progress toward measures will be monitored by the core partners by 
utilizing the Arizona Job Connection website’s reporting capabilities and will be viewed 
by quarter and program year.   
 
b. A description of the actions the local board will take toward becoming or 
remaining a high-performing board, consistent with the factors developed by the 
State board pursuant to section 101(d)(6). 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board is and will continue to be a high-
performing Board.  The local board will administer the Workforce Development

<!-- page 470 -->
62 
 
System to engage economic development, community stakeholders, and a wide range 
of employers in order to analyze economic conditions in the local area.  The purpose 
of this engagement is to obtain knowledge of the local area in order to ensure that 
workforce development activities meet the needs of employers and encourage 
economic growth.  Enhancing communication, coordinating, and collaborating among 
employers, education, and service providers will allow for the development and 
implementation of career pathways needed by adult, dislocated worked, and youth 
participants, particularly those with barriers to employment.  The result of the 
collaboration will allow the local board to successfully meet and exceed performance 
measures negotiated with the state. 
 
An independent evaluation will be conducted annually to review the local workforce 
delivery system and provide feedback on continuous improvements and areas that 
may be of concern to the Local Workforce Development Board.    In addition, 
internal evaluations will be conducted annually by the deputy director to ensure 
proper input of data into the Arizona Job Connection website to ensure accurate 
performance data is captured and reported regularly. 
 
The Local Workforce Development Board members will receive at least six (6) hours 
of training annually regarding the workforce system to keep them apprised of 
changes, promising practices, sector partnership progress, and local considerations. 
 
c. A description of how the local board will ensure the continuous improvement of 
the eligible providers of services through the system and ensure that such 
providers meet the employment needs of local employers, and workers and 
jobseekers. 
The programs provided through Title I, Title ll, Title lll, and Title lV of the 
Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act are tied to negotiated performance 
measures.   Continued success is directly related to meeting or exceeding the 
negotiated performance levels.    
The Local Workforce Development Board will establish a balanced scorecard metrics 
that provides a format to analyze success ratios of each eligible training provider in 
terms of participant engagement including but not limited to: 
 Credential or certification in an in-demand industry and occupation 
 Entered employment 
 Entered employment in a training related in-demand industry and occupation 
 Retention in a job 
 Retention in a training related in-demand industry and occupation 
 Median wage at job entry comparable to the local negotiated level 
 
 Entry into a more advanced course of study to achieve progress towards a career 
path goal

<!-- page 471 -->
63 
 
Eligible training providers will be trained on the negotiated performance measure 
requirements.  Providers that do not meet or exceed the requirements will be asked to 
create a continuous improvement plan that will be monitored by the Local Workforce 
Development Board for progress.      
The eligible service providers will review employer placements.  Business customers 
utilizing work-based learning opportunities that are intended to result in a job will be 
trained and monitored according to the same criteria as eligible training providers.  
This will include on-the-job training, customized training, and incumbent worker 
training opportunities.    
 
d. A description of how one-stop centers are implementing and transitioning to an 
integrated, technology, enabled intake and case management information system 
for programs carried out under this Act and programs carried out by one-stop 
partners. 
 
Title I Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Program and Title lll Employment 
Service providers will use the Arizona Job Connection as its intake and case 
management system and tracking system for service engagement and performance 
outcomes. 
 
A common intake form with a menu of services will be utilized by local area 
welcome teams in order to create a seamless service flow for ARIZONA@WORK 
Job Center customers.  This information is entered into a database to capture data 
regarding customer utilization of the ARIZONA@WORK Job Center. 
 
All core partners are currently located in the ARIZONE@WORK Job Center.  A 
resource room with computers, fax, copier, and telephone is available in the one-stop 
for the use of public, participants, employers, and partner programs.  Employers are 
encouraged to create an enrollment in the Arizona Job Connection system where they 
can post employment opportunities at no cost.  Participants are highly encouraged to 
create an enrollment in the Arizona Job Connection system for resume and job search 
activities.  This creates a connection between the job seeker and employer.   
 
Adult education has installed cameras in the resource room and testing areas in an 
effort to serve customers completing different activities in an efficient manner. 
 
Local Workforce Development Area contact and program information is available 
online through the ARIZONA@WORK website, as well as Navajo, Apache, and Gila 
counties websites. 
 
A universal orientation is being developed and will be implemented to provide 
information regarding all core partners to encourage enrollment in all necessary 
partners when it would benefit the customers.  A universally accepted release of 
information between core partners will be created and implemented to allow for the 
sharing of information for co-enrolled participants as Adult Education and Vocational

<!-- page 472 -->
64 
 
Rehabilitation do not utilize the Arizona Job Connection System.  This will assist 
with ensuring that services are not duplicated among partners. 
 
Due to the geographic and demographics of Navajo, Apache, and Gila Counties, 
technology enabled intake and case management is a critical issue.   Therefore it is 
imperative that all partners communicate information about clients to each other 
through technology based methods such as email, fax, or conference calls to enhance 
gathering the information necessary to affectively provide services to participants.

Section 10:  Complaint and Grievance Procedure 
 
Title l Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Programs 
The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act (WIOA) Title I program provides that 
participants, sub-grantees, subcontractors, employees, and other interested parties may file 
a complaint if they believe that they have been discriminated against, or that the Act, 
regulations, grant and/or other agreements under the Act have created a discriminatory 
violation.   
A compliant must be filed within 180 days from the date of the alleged violation with either: 
Discrimination Complaints  
 
Alleging discrimination complaints on the basis of race, color, religion, sex, national origin, 
age, disability, political affiliation or belief, citizenship or participation in WIOA, must be 
filed within 180 days from the date of the alleged violation with either:

<!-- page 474 -->
66 
 
Non-Criminal Complaints 
 
Alleged non-criminal (program) complaints shall be submitted to the Deputy Director: 
 
Christina Throop, Deputy Director 
ARIZONA@WORK  
Northeastern Arizona Innovative Workforce Solutions  
5515 S Apache Ave. Suite 200 
Globe, AZ   85501 
928- 402-8660 
Christina.throop@navajocountyaz.gov 
 
Non-criminal complaints must be filed within one year of the alleged violation.  Upon 
receipt of the complaint, the following process will be initiated:  
1. Deputy Director Officer or her designee will informally attempt to resolve the issue 
with the complainant. 
2. If complaint is unresolved, a written notice of date, time, and place of a formal 
hearing will be provided to all interested parties.  A hearing officer will be assigned.  
Such hearing shall be within 30 days of the filing. 
3. A formal complaint log must be maintained to indicate date received, hearing date, 
summary of resolution and date resolved. 
4. The written notice is also to advise complainant of their opportunity to present 
evidence and their right to appeal any decision. 
5.    A decision by the hearing officer is to be rendered within sixty (60) days of the 
hearing.  All interested parties are to receive a copy of the final decision that will 
include the basis for the decision. 
6. If the complainant does not receive a decision within sixty (60) days, or receives any 
unsatisfactory decision, he/she may appeal to the next level. 
7. The appeal shall be submitted in writing, within 10 days after the decision, to the 
Governor’s WIOA Administrative entity, The Arizona Department of Economic 
Security (DES) P. O. Box 6123, Site Code 920Z Phoenix, Arizona 85005.  The appeal 
shall contain the hearing officer’s decision along with the complaint and the review 
request. 
8. The DES decision will be released within sixty (60) days of the appeal hearing.  Any 
adjudication by DES is final and there are no further appeals through this 
administrative process. 
 
OJT participants shall utilize the complaint procedures of the employer for whom they work 
concerning work related complaints. Employers shall inform participants of their company's 
complaint procedures.  
 
Employers shall provide a copy of their resolution decision to the Local Workforce 
Development Area Case Manager, upon written request by the complainant.  Such requests

<!-- page 475 -->
67 
 
for review must be submitted within ten (10) days of receipt of the employer’s 
determination. 
 
Vocational Rehabilitation  
 
The Vocational Rehabilitation program provides a variety of services to persons with 
disabilities, with the ultimate goal to prepare for, enter into, or retain employment. 
VR Counselors are available to serve persons with all types of disabilities. Counselors with 
specialized training are available to meet the unique needs of the following populations: 
 Blind, Visually Impaired and Deaf/Hard of hearing 
 Transition Youth 
 Brain Injury and Spinal Cord Injury 
 Behavioral Health 
Appeals Policy 
  
1.  Anytime a decision which affects the provision of Vocational Rehabilitation services that 
has been made by AZRSA staff, AZRSA staff will provide the following information to the 
client or their representative:  
a. Advise the client of their right to request a review of the determination; 
b. Provide a written notice of client rights and appeals options by utilizing the Request 
for Review of Determination form; and  
c. Advise the client of the availability and the right to utilize the Client Assistance 
Program (CAP). 
2.  Notice of client rights and written notice of appeals will be provided in writing by utilizing 
the Request for Review of Determination form to the client, and their representative if 
appropriate, at the following times:  
a. When the client applies for services;  
b. When AZRSA staff makes an eligibility decision; State of Arizona 23 Vocational 
Rehabilitation Policy Manual Last Revision 9/14/2016  
c. When AZRSA staff determines placement into an Order of  
Selection category;  
d. When an Individualized Plan for Employment (IPE) is developed;  
e. At any time that the AZRSA staff makes a decision or intends to reduce, suspend, or 
terminate planned services and/or goods being provided;  
f. When AZRSA staff notifies the client of intent to close the case for any reason; and  
g. Upon denial of Post-Employment Services (PES).  
3. AZRSA staff will advise the client of the following appeals processes available to them.  
a. Informal Resolution;  
b. Administrative Review;  
c. Mediation; and/or  
d. Fair Hearing.

<!-- page 476 -->
68 
 
 4.  If a decision is being disputed, AZRSA staff will ensure that the client, or their 
representative if appropriate, is aware that a formal request for review of a 
determination must be submitted within 15 calendar days of the date that the written 
decision letter was mailed. 
5. Formal written requests are acceptable in the following formats:  
a. Utilizing a Request for Review of Determination; or b. Any other written 
communication which will include:  
i. An explanation of the issue(s) under contention;  
ii. Client, or their representative if appropriate, signature and date; and  
iii. An indication whether the client is willing to resolve the issue(s) through an 
informal review, administrative review, State of Arizona 24 Vocational 
Rehabilitation Policy Manual Last Revision 9/14/2016 mediation, or formal due 
process hearing.  
6. AZRSA staff will direct client who request assistance in completing a written appeal to the 
AZRSA Ombudsman or local CAP office.  
 7.  The AZRSA Ombudsman will schedule a Fair Hearing to occur within 60 days of 
submitting a Request for Review of Determination regardless of the resolution options 
chosen
